The count of Remark~\ref{rem:cubeheuristic} can be carried out exactly for a
Gaussian field, the quadratic approximation of the gauge-fixed theory at weak
coupling, and shows what (D) asks of the gauge-fixed variables.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:gauss}
Let $d\ge1$, $b>0$, $t\ge0$, let $n\ge2$ be even and $V=[0,n)^d\cap\Z^d$.
Given $\eta\in\R^{\Z^d\setminus V}$, let $\gamma_V(\cdot\mid\eta)$ be the
Gaussian law on $\R^V$ with density proportional to
\[
\exp\Bigl(-\frac b2\Bigl[\sum_{\{x,y\}\cap V\ne\emptyset}(\phi_x-\phi_y)^2
+t\sum_{x\in V}\phi_x^2\Bigr]\Bigr),
\]
the first sum over nearest-neighbour pairs, with $\phi=\eta$ outside $V$.
Define $C_{V,j}$, the classes $c$ (residues modulo two) and $K_c$ as above,
with $d_V(\phi,\phi')=\sum_{x\in V}|\phi_x-\phi'_x|$ and $\delta(\eta_j,\eta'_j)
=|\eta_j-\eta'_j|$, and put $N_n=(n/2)^d$. Then $K_c=\alpha_n(t)N_n$ for every
class $c$, where
\[
\alpha_n(t)=1-\frac t{n^d}\,\mathbf 1^{\mathsf T}(t+L_n)^{-1}\mathbf 1
\]
and $L_n$ is the Dirichlet Laplacian of $V$. In particular:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=*]
\item for $t=0$, $K_c=N_n$ for every $n$ and every $b$, so $D(n,\alpha)$
fails for every $\alpha<1$;
\item for $t>0$, $1-\alpha_n(t)\ge(5/6)^d\,tn^2/(tn^2+10d)$; for $d=4$,
$t=m^2a^2$ and $\ell/2\le na\le\ell$ this gives $D(n,\alpha)$ with
$\alpha=1-(5/6)^4(m\ell)^2/((m\ell)^2+160)$, uniformly in $a$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $A=t+L_n$ and $G=A^{-1}$, and let $B$ be the matrix with $B_{xj}=1$ when
$x\in V$ and $j\notin V$ are neighbours. The law $\gamma_V(\cdot\mid\eta)$ has
covariance $b^{-1}G$ and mean $GB\eta$. The matrix $A$ has nonpositive
off-diagonal entries and is positive definite, so $G\ge0$ entrywise. Changing
$\eta_j$ by $\Delta$ translates the law by $\Delta\,GB_j$, where $B_j$ is the
column of $j$. The coupling by translation gives $W_1^{d_V}\le|\Delta|
\sum_x(GB_j)_x$, and the $1$-Lipschitz function $\sum_x\phi_x$ gives the
reverse inequality, so $C_{V,j}=\mathbf1^{\mathsf T}GB_j$. Since
$B\mathbf1=L_n\mathbf1$ (both count the exterior neighbours of each site),
\[
\sum_{j\notin V}C_{V,j}=\mathbf1^{\mathsf T}GL_n\mathbf1
=\mathbf1^{\mathsf T}\mathbf1-t\,\mathbf1^{\mathsf T}G\mathbf1
=n^d\alpha_n(t).
\]
The reflection $x_i\mapsto n-1-x_i$ preserves $V$ and the coefficients and,
as $n$ is even, exchanges the two residues of the coordinate $x_i$; so the
$2^d$ class sums are equal, and each is $n^d\alpha_n(t)/2^d=\alpha_n(t)N_n$.
This proves the formula and (i).

For (ii) let $v(i)=(i+1)(n-i)$ for $0\le i<n$ and $\psi(x)=\prod_iv(x_i)$.
The one-dimensional Dirichlet Laplacian maps $v$ to the constant $2$, so with
$s_1=\sum_iv(i)=n(n+1)(n+2)/6$ and $s_2=\sum_iv(i)^2=n(n+1)(n+2)(n^2+2n+2)/30$,
\[
\mathbf1^{\mathsf T}\psi=s_1^d,\qquad
\psi^{\mathsf T}A\psi=ts_2^d+2ds_1s_2^{d-1}.
\]
By the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality for the form of $A^{-1}$,
$\mathbf1^{\mathsf T}G\mathbf1\ge(\mathbf1^{\mathsf T}\psi)^2/\psi^{\mathsf T}
A\psi$, hence
\[
1-\alpha_n(t)\ge\Bigl(\frac{s_1^2}{ns_2}\Bigr)^d\frac t{t+2ds_1/s_2}
\ge\Bigl(\frac56\Bigr)^d\frac{tn^2}{tn^2+10d},
\]
since $s_1^2/(ns_2)=\frac56(n^2+3n+2)/(n^2+2n+2)\ge\frac56$ and
$2ds_1/s_2=10d/(n^2+2n+2)\le10d/n^2$. With $d=4$ and $tn^2=m^2(na)^2\ge
(m\ell)^2/4$ the last fraction is at least $(m\ell)^2/((m\ell)^2+160)$.
\end{proof}

The Gaussian field is not Yang--Mills theory, and the proposition is a
benchmark for the condition, not a statement about the Wilson measure. It
shows that the averaged cube condition is met at a fixed physical scale by a
field with a mass and fails exactly, at every size and every stiffness, for
a massless one. Small local fluctuations alone therefore give nothing: in the
massless Gaussian field on $\Z^d$, $d\ge3$, the variance of
$\phi_x-\phi_{x+e_1}$ is $1/(db)$,
which tends to zero as $b\to\infty$, and still $K_c=N_n$. A proof of (D)
along the tuning has to produce in the gauge-fixed variables the loss that
the parameter $t$ supplies here, from the Wilson action alone.

The quadratic approximation of the forest gauge itself, with the plaquette
action in place of the Laplacian, is no better than the massless field of
Proposition~\ref{prop:gauss}(i).

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:gaussforest}
Let $b>0$, let $n$ be even and $V$ the cube of Definition~\ref{def:cube}.
For real functions $\varphi$ on the links of $\Z^4$ that vanish on $F$, let
$(d\varphi)_p$ be the sum of $\varphi$ around the plaquette $p$ with the signs
of its orientation. Given $\eta\in\R^{A\setminus V}$, let
$\gamma_V(\cdot\mid\eta)$ be the law on $\R^V$ with density proportional to
\[
\exp\Bigl(-\frac b2\sum_{p\cap V\ne\emptyset}(d\varphi)_p^2\Bigr),
\]
with $\varphi=\eta$ on $A\setminus V$ and $\varphi=0$ on $F$, and define
$C_{V,j}$ and $K_c$ as above with $d_V(\varphi,\varphi')=\sum_{i\in V}
|\varphi_i-\varphi'_i|$ and $\delta(\eta_j,\eta'_j)=|\eta_j-\eta'_j|$. Then
\[
\sum_cK_c\ge|V|=48N_n ,
\]
so $K_c\ge N_n$ for some class $c$, and $D(n,\alpha)$ fails for every
$\alpha<1$, at every $n$ and every $b$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\emph{The laws.} The quadratic form $\sum_{p\cap V\ne\emptyset}(d\varphi)_p^2$
with $\varphi=0$ outside $V$ vanishes only at $\varphi=0$: the argument of
Corollary~\ref{cor:cubefails}, written additively, gives a function $h$ on
the sites with $\varphi_{(x,\mu)}=h(x)-h(x+e_\mu)$, constant outside the box
and along the lines of $F$, hence constant. So $\gamma_V(\cdot\mid\eta)$ is a
Gaussian law whose covariance does not depend on $\eta$ and whose mean is
$\sum_j\eta_ju_j$ for some vectors $u_j\in\R^V$. Two exteriors that differ
in finitely many links give translates of one law by
$t=\sum_j(\eta'_j-\eta_j)u_j$; the coupling by translation and the
$1$-Lipschitz function $\sum_i\operatorname{sign}(t_i)\varphi_i$ show that
their distance is $\|t\|_1$. In particular $C_{V,j}=\|u_j\|_1$, and
\[
W_1^{d_V}\bigl(\gamma_V(\cdot\mid\eta),\gamma_V(\cdot\mid\eta')\bigr)\le
\sum_jC_{V,j}\,|\eta'_j-\eta_j| .
\]

\emph{Lines.} By Lemma~\ref{lem:structure}(i) the lines of $F$ are straight,
disjoint and cover the sites, and the two ends of an active link $(x,\mu)$
lie on different lines, since the line through $x$ has direction
$\sigma(x)\ne\mu$. For a line $\ell$ let $h_\ell$
be the indicator of its sites and $(dh_\ell)_{(x,\mu)}=h_\ell(x)-h_\ell(x+e_\mu)$.
Then $dh_\ell$ vanishes on $F$ and around every plaquette, so the density is
invariant under $\varphi\mapsto\varphi+dh_\ell$, and
$\gamma_V(\cdot\mid dh_\ell)$ is $\gamma_V(\cdot\mid0)$ translated by the
restriction of $dh_\ell$ to $V$. Hence
\[
\sum_{i\in V}|(dh_\ell)_i|\le\sum_{j\in A\setminus V}C_{V,j}\,|(dh_\ell)_j| .
\]
Here $|(dh_\ell)_e|=1$ when exactly one end of $e$ lies on $\ell$ and $0$
otherwise, so $\sum_\ell|(dh_\ell)_e|=2$ for every active link $e$, the sum
running over the finitely many lines that meet a link entering the
inequality. Summing over these lines gives $2|V|\le2\sum_jC_{V,j}=
2\sum_cK_c$.
\end{proof}

The proof uses only that gauge transformations constant on the lines of $F$
move the whole cube while changing few boundary links, which is the
behaviour of gauges of axial type anticipated in
Remark~\ref{rem:cubeheuristic}. The class sums themselves, computed exactly
for $n\le8$, lie between $1.57$ and $11.24$ times $N_n$ and
increase with $n$. Below the correlation length the theory is weakly
coupled, and the quadratic approximation is its leading order there. A proof
of (D) along the tuning therefore has to show that the nonperturbative
regime reduces the cube coefficients of the forest gauge below $N_n$, or
(D) has to be posed for other variables.

The lines of $F$ are not what makes the argument work. A constant field,
corrected by a periodic gauge transformation so that it vanishes on the
fixed links, costs no action and changes every exterior link by a bounded
amount, and this happens in every gauge fixed on an invariant forest.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:gaussevery}
Let $b>0$ and let $F$ be a set of links of $\Z^4$, invariant under the
translations by $2\Z^4$, that contains no closed path and whose trees are
infinite and contain every site. Let $A$ be the set of the other links, $V$
the set of links $(x,\mu)\in A$ with $x\in[0,n)^4$, $n\ge2$ even, and define
the classes, $\gamma_V(\cdot\mid\eta)$, $C_{V,j}$ and $K_c$ as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:gaussforest} with this $F$. Then there are numbers
$a_c\ge0$, not all zero, with
\[
\sum_ca_cK_c\ge N_n\sum_ca_c ,
\]
so $K_c\ge N_n$ for some class $c$, and $D(n,\alpha)$ fails for every
$\alpha<1$, at every $n$ and every $b$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
\emph{The laws.} Let $\varphi$ vanish outside $V$ and on $F$, with
$(d\varphi)_p=0$ for every plaquette $p$ that meets $V$. The other plaquettes
contain no link of $V$, so $d\varphi=0$ on every plaquette, and
$\varphi=dh$ for a function $h$ on the sites. Since $\varphi$ vanishes
outside a box and the complement of a box is connected, $h$ is constant
there, and we take that constant to be $0$. As $\varphi$ vanishes on $F$, $h$
is constant on every tree of $F$; each tree is infinite and meets the
complement of the box, so $h=0$ and $\varphi=0$. Hence, as in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:gaussforest}, $\gamma_V(\cdot\mid\eta)$ is a
nondegenerate Gaussian law with mean $\sum_j\eta_ju_j$, where only the links
$j$ that share a plaquette with $V$ contribute, and $C_{V,j}=\|u_j\|_1$.

\emph{A flat field.} For $c\in\R^4$ and a function $h$ on $\Z^4$ of period
two in each coordinate put $\psi_{(x,\mu)}=c_\mu+h(x)-h(x+e_\mu)$. Then
$d\psi=0$ on every plaquette and $\psi$ is invariant under $2\Z^4$; if
$\psi=0$, adding $\psi$ at $(x,\mu)$ and $(x+e_\mu,\mu)$ gives $2c_\mu=0$,
and then $h$ is constant. These fields form a space of dimension
$16-1+4=19$. Let $f$ be the number of orbits of $2\Z^4$ in $F$. The box
$[0,2m)^4$ has $16m^4$ sites, and the links of $F$ with both ends in it form
a forest, so there are at most $16m^4-1$ of them, while each orbit
contributes at least $(m-1)^4$; letting $m\to\infty$ gives $f\le16$. Vanishing
on $F$ is therefore at most $16$ linear conditions, and some $\psi\ne0$ of
this form vanishes on $F$. It is nonzero on some link of $A$. Put
$a_c=|\psi_j|$ for $j\in c$.

\emph{The inequality.} Let $\eta$ be the restriction of $\psi$ to
$A\setminus V$. The field equal to $\psi$ on $V$ and to $\eta$ outside gives
the quadratic form its least value $0$, so by nondegeneracy the mean of
$\gamma_V(\cdot\mid\eta)$ is the restriction of $\psi$ to $V$, and
$\psi_i=\sum_j\psi_j(u_j)_i$ for $i\in V$. Hence
\[
\sum_{i\in V}|\psi_i|\le\sum_{j\in A\setminus V}C_{V,j}|\psi_j|=\sum_ca_cK_c .
\]
A class consists of the active links with a given direction and a given
residue of the base point modulo two, so it meets $V$ in $N_n$ links, and
the left side is $N_n\sum_ca_c>0$. So $K_c\ge N_n$ for some class with
$a_c>0$.
\end{proof}

For the direction field of Table~\ref{tab:sigma} the flat fields vanishing
on $F$ form a space of dimension $7$, and for the axial field $\sigma=0$ one
of dimension $10$. The proposition is weaker than
Proposition~\ref{prop:gaussforest} for the forest gauge, which bounds the
sum over all classes, and it says nothing about two kinds of condition. A
mass gives the constant fields an action, which is why
Proposition~\ref{prop:gauss} distinguishes $t>0$ from $t=0$. The constrained
condition $D_{\rm c}$ of \cite[Theorem~334]{BBLR26} holds the block
averages fixed, and a constant field changes them. Within the quadratic
approximation no gauge fixed on an invariant forest gives (D); the loss has
to come from the Wilson action beyond its quadratic part, or (D) has to be
posed for variables, such as the block averages, that a constant field
moves.

\begin{remark}\label{rem:routes}
Five other routes stop at (D) or (E), or earlier.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)},leftmargin=*]
\item \emph{Disjoint blocks.} The block criterion of
Lemma~\ref{lem:blockcomparison} with a partition into cubes of side $n$
requires, for a link on a face, that its influence on the neighbouring cube
be less than one. In the count of Remark~\ref{rem:cubeheuristic} that
influence is of order $\xi$, which grows like $1/a$. Averaging over the $N_n$
translates divides it by the number of translates on whose boundary the link
lies, a factor of order $1/n$, and this is why (D) is stated for averaged
cubes.
\item \emph{Rescaling a fixed lattice gap.} Any bound uniform in lattice
units, such as those of Theorems~\ref{thm:su2}--\ref{thm:su3blocks}, gives
trivial continuum two-point functions~\cite{BB26b}; rescaling it produces
no gap.
\item \emph{Assuming the gap.} Hypothesis~R of~\cite{BB26b} assumes the gap
in physical units together with the limit. Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer}
replaces the gap part by the cube estimate (D), which concerns one box and
one coupling at each spacing.
\item \emph{Computation.} At a fixed coupling and a fixed small $n$,
$D(n,\alpha)$ is a finite set of Kantorovich bounds and could in principle be
evaluated as in Remark~\ref{rem:su3cover}; for
$n=2$ and $\SU(2)$ the block already has $48$ links and $144$ real variables.
(D) asks for it at infinitely many couplings with $n\to\infty$, which no
finite computation covers.
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\subsection{The ratio, the strong-coupling range and the cube estimate}\label{ssec:ratiocube}

This subsection states the open input of Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit}
exactly, proves that it fails along every sequence of couplings in the range
where the paper has a gap, shows that the hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer} holds along some of these sequences and therefore
cannot imply the input, and replaces the input by a condition that is
necessary and sufficient and asks only for an upper bound on an energy and a
lower bound on one overlap.

The notation is that of Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit}: translation invariant
states $\omega_k$ at couplings $\beta_k$, with transfer matrices $\mathbb T_k$
and vacua $\Omega_k$ (Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}), the centred flowed packet
$A_k$ of a nonzero $f_0\in C_c(\R^4)$ supported in $\{t>0\}$, and
$C_k(t)=\omega_k(\vartheta(\bar A_k)\tau_{t/a_k}A_k)$ for $t\in a_k\Z_{\ge0}$,
with $C_k(0)>0$. For $u\ge0$ and $\sigma>0$ in $a_k\Z$ put
\[
\rho_k(u,\sigma)=\frac{C_k(2u+\sigma)}{C_k(2u)} ,
\]
so that $\rho(\sigma)$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit} is a limit of
$\rho_k(0,\sigma)$. Let $f_{0,u}=f_0(\cdot-(u,0))$; for $u/a_k\in\Z$ its packet
is $\tau_{u/a_k}A_k$, and $\Psi_{k,u}=C_k(2u)^{-1/2}\Psi_{\tau_{u/a_k}A_k}$ is
the corresponding unit vector. For dyadic $u\ge0$ consider
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(N$_u$)},leftmargin=*]
\item $\liminf_{k\to\infty}\rho_k(u,\sigma)>0$ for some dyadic $\sigma>0$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:ratioexact}
\textup{(i)} $C_k(t)>0$ for every $t\in a_k\Z_{\ge0}$, and $\log C_k$ is
convex and nonincreasing on $a_k\Z_{\ge0}$.

\textup{(ii)} $0<\rho_k(u,\sigma)\le1$; $\rho_k(u,\sigma)$ is nondecreasing in
$u$ and nonincreasing in $\sigma$, and
$\rho_k(u,\sigma')\ge\rho_k(u,\sigma)^{\sigma'/\sigma}$ for $\sigma'\ge\sigma$.

\textup{(iii)} For $u\ge0$ dyadic, Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit} applies to
$f_{0,u}$, with reflected norm $C_k(2u)$ and ratio $\rho_k(u,\sigma)$. Every
limit it produces along subsequences is nontrivial if and only if
$(\mathrm N_u)$ holds, and some limit is nontrivial if and only if
$\limsup_k\rho_k(u,\sigma)>0$. If $(\mathrm N_u)$ holds for one $\sigma$, it
holds for every dyadic $\sigma>0$, and $(\mathrm N_u)$ implies
$(\mathrm N_{u'})$ for $u'\ge u$.

\textup{(iv)} Let $\mu_{k,u}$ be the spectral measure, in energies
$E=-a_k^{-1}\log\lambda$, of $\mathbb T_k$ at $\Psi_{k,u}$. If
$\rho_k(u,\sigma)\ge c>0$, then $\mu_{k,u}([0,\Lambda])\ge c/2$ with
$\Lambda=\sigma^{-1}\log(2/c)$; if $\mu_{k,u}([0,\Lambda])\ge v$, then
$\rho_k(u,\sigma)\ge ve^{-\sigma\Lambda}$. So $(\mathrm N_u)$ holds if and only
if a fixed fraction of the spectral mass of $\Psi_{k,u}$ stays at bounded
physical energy, uniformly in $k$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) The function $f_0$ is supported in $\{t>0\}$, so the lattice points with
$f_0(a_kx)\ne0$ have $x_0\ge1$, and $A_k=\sum_{n\ge1}\tau_nB_n$ is a finite
sum in which $B_n$ is a function of the spatial links of the slice at time
$0$, by Proposition~\ref{prop:spatialflow}(i). In the reconstruction~\cite{OS73,OS75,OSei78}, as in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit}, $\Psi_{\tau_nB}=\mathbb T_k^n\Psi_B$, hence
$\Psi_{A_k}=\mathbb T_k\xi$ with $\xi=\sum_n\mathbb T_k^{n-1}\Psi_{B_n}$. The
transfer matrix is self-adjoint and positive~\cite{OSei78,Lus77,Sei82}, and
its range is orthogonal to its kernel,
so the spectral measure $\nu_k$ of $\mathbb T_k$ at
$\Psi_k=C_k(0)^{-1/2}\Psi_{A_k}$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:spectralmeasure}) has no atom
at $0$. Therefore $C_k(t)/C_k(0)=\int\lambda^{t/a_k}d\nu_k>0$. Convexity and
monotonicity are Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}(ii).

(ii) The first claims follow from (i). On a lattice, a convex function has
chord slopes that increase in both end points; this gives monotonicity in
$u$, and, comparing the chords $[2u,2u+\sigma]$ and $[2u,2u+\sigma']$, the
power bound. Monotonicity in $\sigma$ is that of $C_k$.

(iii) Translation invariance and $\vartheta\tau_u=\tau_{-u}\vartheta$ give,
with times written in lattice steps,
\[
\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(\tau_u\bar A_k)\,\tau_{t+u}A_k\bigr)
=\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(\bar A_k)\,\tau_{t+2u}A_k\bigr),
\]
which gives the reflected norm and the ratio. The function $f_{0,u}$ is nonzero and
supported in $\{t>u\}$. If $\liminf_k\rho_k(u,\sigma)=0$, a subsequence has
$\rho_k(u,\sigma)\to0$, and a refinement given by
Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit}(ii) has a trivial limit by part (iv) of that
theorem; if the $\liminf$ is at least $c>0$, every limit has $\rho(\sigma)\ge
c$ and is nontrivial. The statement with $\limsup$ is the last part of
Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit}(iv). Independence of $\sigma$ follows from (ii),
by the power bound for $\sigma'\ge\sigma$ and by monotonicity for
$\sigma'\le\sigma$, and the last claim from monotonicity in $u$.

(iv) The vector $\Psi_{k,u}$ has no spectral weight at $\lambda=0$, by the
proof of (i), and $\rho_k(u,\sigma)=\int e^{-\sigma E}d\mu_{k,u}$. This
integral is at most $\mu_{k,u}([0,\Lambda])+e^{-\sigma\Lambda}$ and at least
$e^{-\sigma\Lambda}\mu_{k,u}([0,\Lambda])$.
\end{proof}

The open input of Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit} is therefore exactly
$(\mathrm N_u)$ for one packet $f_0$ and one dyadic $u\ge0$. It is a uniformity
statement and nothing else: at each fixed $k$ the ratio is positive by (i).
The required uniformity is in $k$ along the tuning, at fixed physical
separations $u$ and $\sigma$, so that $\sigma/a_k\to\infty$ lattice steps;
the constant may depend on $G$, $\Phi$, $\tau$, $f_0$, $u$, $\sigma$ and on the
tuning, and nothing is required uniformly in $f_0$ or in $\tau$. The ratio
does not change when $A_k$ is multiplied by a constant, so the renormalisation
$Z_k$ plays no role in it. The case $u=0$ is the strongest and implies all the
others. Proposition~\ref{prop:flowshift}(i) assumes $(\mathrm N_0)$ and
renormalises the packet shifted by $\sigma/2$. The hypothesis of
\cite[Lemma~462]{BBLR26} is $(\mathrm N_{s/4})$ at $\sigma=s/2$; it is
weaker than $(\mathrm N_0)$, as the example after
Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio} shows, and it suffices for a nontrivial
limit of the packet shifted by $s/4$. Granted the convergence asked for in (E), the
clause of (E) that the limit is not that of the vacuum alone is, for the
flowed packets, $(\mathrm N_u)$ for one $f_0$ and one $u$; the convergence of
the whole sequence, Euclidean invariance and the higher correlations
remain~\cite{JW06}.
No bound is required at one lattice step: the first-step energy
$-a_k^{-1}\log\rho_k(u,a_k)$ may diverge while $(\mathrm N_u)$ holds, as
Remark~\ref{rem:compositeslice} shows for the slice field of the unflowed
magnetic energy.

\emph{The strong-coupling range.} In the range where the paper proves a
gap, the ratio has two-sided bounds in lattice units, and the upper one rules
out every continuum tuning there. Put
\[
m_0=-\tfrac12\log0.9936 ,
\]
so that $m_0\ge0.0032$ by $-\log(1-x)\ge x$.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:strongratio}
Let $G=\SU(2)$, let $\beta_{W,k}\in[0,0.348]$ be any couplings, and let
$\omega_k$ be the states of Theorems~\ref{thm:su2} and~\ref{thm:blocks}. Then
for every $\Phi$, $\tau$ and $f_0$ with $C_k(0)>0$, every dyadic $u\ge0$ and
$\sigma>0$, and every $k$ with $u/a_k,\sigma/a_k\in\Z$,
\[
\rho_k(0,a_k)^{\sigma/a_k}\le\rho_k(u,\sigma)\le e^{-m_0\sigma/a_k} .
\]
Consequently every limit of Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:flowlimit}} along these
couplings, for every packet $f_{0,u}$, is trivial, and if $(\mathrm N_u)$ holds
for some packet along a tuning $(\beta_{W,k})$ of $\SU(2)$, then
$\beta_{W,k}>0.348$ for all large $k$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:blocks} the transfer matrix of $\omega_k$ has gap
$m_k\ge-\frac12\log\alpha(\beta_{W,k})$ on $\Omega_k^\perp$, and $\alpha$ is
increasing with $\alpha(0.348)\le0.9936$, so $m_k\ge m_0$. The vector
$\Psi_{k,u}$ is a unit vector orthogonal to $\Omega_k$, because $A_k$ is
centred, and $\mathbb T_k$ preserves $\Omega_k^\perp$, so
$\rho_k(u,\sigma)=\ip{\Psi_{k,u}}{\mathbb T_k^{\sigma/a_k}\Psi_{k,u}}\le
e^{-m_0\sigma/a_k}$. For the lower bound, $\rho_k(u,\sigma)\ge\rho_k(0,\sigma)$
by Proposition~\ref{prop:ratioexact}(ii), and Jensen's inequality for the
convex function $\lambda^{\sigma/a_k}$ and the probability measure $\nu_k$ of
the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:ratioexact}(i) gives
$\rho_k(0,\sigma)=\int\lambda^{\sigma/a_k}d\nu_k\ge(\int\lambda\,d\nu_k)^{
\sigma/a_k}=\rho_k(0,a_k)^{\sigma/a_k}$, with $\rho_k(0,a_k)>0$ by
Proposition~\ref{prop:ratioexact}(i). The upper bound tends to zero as
$a_k\to0$, which gives triviality by Proposition~\ref{prop:ratioexact}(iii).
If $(\mathrm N_u)$ held along a tuning with $\beta_{W,k}\le0.348$ for
infinitely many $k$, the upper bound along those $k$ would contradict it.
\end{proof}

Writing $\rho_k(u,\sigma)=e^{-\varepsilon_k\sigma/a_k}$, the theorem says
$m_0\le\varepsilon_k\le-\log\rho_k(0,a_k)$: both ends are rates in lattice
units, and $(\mathrm N_u)$ asks for $\varepsilon_k=O(a_k)$. Reflection
positivity gives the positivity of the lower end, through the range of
$\mathbb T_k$, and the transfer-matrix gap gives the upper bound; neither
bears on the size of $\varepsilon_k/a_k$ except to exclude the range. The size
of $\rho_k(0,a_k)$ is not estimated here; any estimate of it at strong
coupling is in lattice units, and it is at most $e^{-m_0}$. The same holds for
every compact simple $G$ and couplings in $[0,\beta_1]$ with
$N\beta_1<\frac2{29}\kappa_G$, with $m_0=m_G(\beta_1)$ of
Theorem~\ref{thm:groups}; for fixed $\beta$ this is
Proposition~\ref{prop:treecritical}(i). So the strong-coupling range bears on
the continuum tuning only negatively: a tuning with a nontrivial limit leaves
it for good. That the tuning has to go to $\beta_W\to\infty$ is the
expectation from asymptotic freedom and is not proved here.

\emph{The cube estimate in the same range.} The hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer} holds at fixed strong coupling with cubes of fixed
physical size, and with every $\alpha$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:cubecompatible}
\textup{(i)} Suppose $D(n_k,\alpha_k)$ holds at $\beta_k$ with $n_ka_k\le\ell$.
Then $\rho_k(u,\sigma)\le\alpha_k^{\sigma/(2\ell)}$ for every $u$ and
$\sigma$. Hence $(\mathrm N_u)$ with constant $c$ at $\sigma$ forces
$\alpha_k\ge c^{2\ell/\sigma}$ for large $k$, and the physical mass of
Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:transfer}} obeys $m_*\le\sigma^{-1}\log(1/c)$.

\textup{(ii)} Let $G=\SU(2)$, $0\le\beta_W<\frac13$ fixed, $\ell>0$, and
$n_k=2\lfloor\ell/(2a_k)\rfloor$. For every $\alpha\in(0,1)$, $D(n_k,\alpha)$
holds at $\beta_W$ for all $k$ with $n_k\ge2$ and
$5\beta_W\le\alpha(1-3\beta_W)n_k$, and $n_ka_k\le\ell$. So the hypothesis of
Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:transfer}} holds along the constant tuning, from
some $k$ on, with every $\alpha\in(0,1)$, while every limit of
Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:flowlimit}} is trivial.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) By Theorem~\ref{thm:onescale} the gap of $\mathbb T_k$ on
$\Omega_k^\perp$ is at least $\log(1/\alpha_k)/(2n_k)\ge a_k\log(1/\alpha_k)
/(2\ell)$, and $\rho_k(u,\sigma)\le e^{-m_k\sigma/a_k}$ as in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:strongratio}. The bound on $m_*$ is the case $\alpha_k=
\alpha$, since then $\alpha^{\sigma/(2\ell)}=e^{-m_*\sigma}$.

(ii) Proposition~\ref{prop:cubesmall} gives $D(n,\alpha)$ for every
$\alpha\ge\hat\alpha_n$, and $\hat\alpha_n\le5\beta_W/((1-3\beta_W)n)$.
Triviality is Theorem~\ref{thm:strongratio}.
\end{proof}

Part (i) shows that (D) is itself a triviality statement when its constant
improves along the tuning: if $\alpha_k\to0$ with $n_ka_k\le\ell$, every limit
is trivial. Along the constant tuning of part (ii) with $\beta_W>0$ this gives
$\rho_k(u,\sigma)\le(5\beta_W/((1-3\beta_W)n_k))^{\sigma/(2\ell)}$, a power of
$a_k$, from the cube estimate alone. Part (ii) shows that no argument that
uses (D) only through the hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer}, or through
Theorem~\ref{thm:onescale}, can give $(\mathrm N_u)$: that hypothesis is
satisfied by Wilson measures whose continuum limits are trivial. Statement
(D) of Sec.~\ref{sec:inputs} adds that $\beta\to\infty$ along the tuning, and an
implication that uses this and the weak-coupling structure besides (D) is not
excluded; it would use information that (D) does not contain.

\emph{Trial states.} The input is equivalent to the existence of states of
bounded physical energy with a fixed overlap with the packet.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:trial}
Let $\mathbb T$ be self-adjoint with $0\le\mathbb T\le1$, let $a>0$ and let
$\Psi$ be a unit vector.

\textup{(a)} Let $\chi$ be a unit vector with $\ip\chi{(1-\mathbb T)\chi}\le
\kappa a$ and $|\ip\chi\Psi|\ge v>0$. Then for $\sigma\ge2a$,
\[
\ip\Psi{\mathbb T^{\sigma/a}\Psi}\ge\Bigl(v-\sqrt{\kappa\sigma/2}\Bigr)_+^2 ,
\]
and if $a\Lambda\le1$ with $\Lambda=8\kappa/v^2$, the spectral projection
$P=\mathbf1_{[e^{-a\Lambda},1]}(\mathbb T)$ has $\|P\Psi\|\ge v/2$.

\textup{(b)} Conversely, if $\ip\Psi{\mathbb T^{\sigma/a}\Psi}\ge c>0$ with
$\sigma/a\in\Z_{>0}$, put $\Lambda=\sigma^{-1}\log(2/c)$ and
$P=\mathbf1_{[e^{-a\Lambda},1]}(\mathbb T)$. Then $\chi=P\Psi/\|P\Psi\|$ is a
unit vector with $\ip\chi{(1-\mathbb T)\chi}\le a\Lambda$ and
$\ip\chi\Psi=\|P\Psi\|\ge(c/2)^{1/2}$. If $\mathbb T\Omega=\Omega$ and
$\Psi\perp\Omega$, then $\chi\perp\Omega$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) Let $m=\sigma/(2a)\ge1$ and let $\nu_\chi$ be the spectral measure at
$\chi$. For $\lambda\in[0,1]$, $0\le1-\lambda^m\le m(1-\lambda)$ by
Bernoulli's inequality, so
\[
\|(1-\mathbb T^m)\chi\|^2=\int(1-\lambda^m)^2d\nu_\chi\le
m\int(1-\lambda)\,d\nu_\chi\le\frac{\kappa\sigma}2 .
\]
Hence $\|\mathbb T^m\Psi\|\ge|\ip{\mathbb T^m\chi}\Psi|\ge|\ip\chi\Psi|-
\|(1-\mathbb T^m)\chi\|\ge v-\sqrt{\kappa\sigma/2}$, and
$\ip\Psi{\mathbb T^{\sigma/a}\Psi}=\|\mathbb T^m\Psi\|^2$. For the projection,
$1-\lambda>1-e^{-a\Lambda}\ge a\Lambda/(1+a\Lambda)\ge a\Lambda/2$ off
$[e^{-a\Lambda},1]$, so $\|(1-P)\chi\|^2\le2\kappa/\Lambda=v^2/4$, and
$\|P\Psi\|\ge|\ip\chi{P\Psi}|\ge|\ip\chi\Psi|-\|(1-P)\chi\|\ge v/2$.

(b) With $\nu$ the spectral measure at $\Psi$,
$c\le\int\lambda^{\sigma/a}d\nu\le\|P\Psi\|^2+e^{-\sigma\Lambda}=\|P\Psi\|^2+
c/2$. On the range of $P$, $1-\mathbb T\le1-e^{-a\Lambda}\le a\Lambda$. Since
$P\Omega=\Omega$, $\ip\Omega{P\Psi}=\ip\Omega\Psi=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:trial}
$(\mathrm N_u)$ holds if and only if there are $\kappa,v>0$ and unit vectors
$\chi_k$ in the reconstructed spaces of the $\omega_k$ such that, for all
large $k$,
\[
\ip{\chi_k}{(1-\mathbb T_k)\chi_k}\le\kappa a_k,\qquad
|\ip{\chi_k}{\Psi_{k,u}}|\ge v .
\]
Then $\rho_k(u,\sigma)\ge(v-\sqrt{\kappa\sigma/2})_+^2$ for $\sigma\ge2a_k$,
and the $\chi_k$ may be taken of the form $\Psi_{X_k}/\|\Psi_{X_k}\|$ for
gauge-invariant local functions $X_k$ at positive times, at the cost of
replacing $(\kappa,v)$ by $(2\kappa,v/2)$. If moreover $D(n_k,\alpha)$ holds
with $n_ka_k\le\ell$, then $m_*=\log(1/\alpha)/(2\ell)\le8\kappa/v^2$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Sufficiency is Lemma~\ref{lem:trial}(a) with $\Psi=\Psi_{k,u}$, $a=a_k$, and
Proposition~\ref{prop:ratioexact}(ii) extends the bound from small $\sigma$ to
every $\sigma$. Necessity is Lemma~\ref{lem:trial}(b) with
$\kappa=\sigma^{-1}\log(2/c)$ and $v=(c/2)^{1/2}$. The vectors $\Psi_X$ with
$X$ local are dense (Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}), and both conditions are
continuous in $\chi_k$ for fixed $k$, $1-\mathbb T_k$ being bounded. Under
$D(n_k,\alpha)$, Theorem~\ref{thm:onescale} puts the spectral measure of
$\Psi_{k,u}\perp\Omega_k$ in $[0,e^{-a_km_*}]$; if $m_*>8\kappa/v^2$, the
projection of Lemma~\ref{lem:trial}(a) annihilates $\Psi_{k,u}$ for large $k$,
which is impossible.
\end{proof}

For $\chi_k=\Psi_{X_k}/\|\Psi_{X_k}\|$ the two conditions are statements about
lattice correlations:
\[
\begin{split}
&\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(\bar X_k)X_k\bigr)-\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(\bar
X_k)\tau_1X_k\bigr)\le\kappa a_k\,\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(\bar X_k)X_k\bigr),\\
&\bigl|\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(\bar X_k)\tau_{u/a_k}A_k\bigr)\bigr|^2\ge v^2\,
\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(\bar X_k)X_k\bigr)\,C_k(2u).
\end{split}
\]
The first is an upper bound on a one-slab Dirichlet form, which is the
direction of variational and perturbative estimates and the opposite of a
mass gap; the second is a lower bound on one correlation. The choice
$\chi_k=\Psi_{k,u}$ is Proposition~\ref{prop:flowshift}(ii), and the order-two
tree and saturation bounds give it by Proposition~\ref{prop:treeenergy}(iii);
the corollary shows that the trial vector need not be the packet itself.

\emph{A soft component of the slice field.} For product packets the trial
vector can be built from a part of the slice field of
Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}, and the conditions become one first-step
bound for that part and one bound for the rest at the earliest time of the
packet. Let $f_0=g\otimes h$ with $g\ge0$ supported in $[t_0,t_1]$, and let
$u_k$, $n_0$, $n_1$ be as in Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:softhard}
Suppose that for all large $k$ there is a decomposition
$u_k=u^{\mathrm s}_k+u^{\mathrm h}_k$ with $\mathbb T_k^{n_0}u^{\mathrm s}_k\ne0$
and, for constants $\kappa>0$ and $\theta\in[0,1)$ with $\kappa a_k\le\frac12$,
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(S\arabic*)},leftmargin=*]
\item $\ip{u^{\mathrm s}_k}{(1-\mathbb T_k)u^{\mathrm s}_k}\le\kappa a_k
\|u^{\mathrm s}_k\|^2$;
\item $\|\mathbb T_k^{n_0}u^{\mathrm h}_k\|\le\theta e^{-2\kappa(t_1-t_0)}
\|\mathbb T_k^{n_0}u^{\mathrm s}_k\|$.
\end{enumerate}
Then the hypothesis of Corollary~\textup{\ref{cor:trial}} holds for $u=0$
with $\kappa e^{4\kappa(t_1-t_0)}$ in place of $\kappa$ and
$v=(1-\theta)/(1+\theta)$; in particular $(\mathrm N_0)$ holds and
\[
\frac{C_k(\sigma)}{C_k(0)}\ge\Bigl(\frac{1-\theta}{1+\theta}-
\sqrt{\tfrac12\kappa\sigma e^{4\kappa(t_1-t_0)}}\Bigr)_+^2
\]
for $\sigma\ge2a_k$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio},
$\Psi_{A_k}=W(\mathbb T_k)u_k$ with $W(\lambda)=\sum_nw_n\lambda^n$, $w_n\ge0$,
$w_n=0$ unless $n_0\le n\le n_1$, and $\|w\|_1\lambda^{n_1}\le W(\lambda)\le
\|w\|_1\lambda^{n_0}$ on $[0,1]$, with $\|w\|_1>0$ because $C_k(0)>0$. Write
$\nu$ for the spectral measure at $u^{\mathrm s}_k$ and
$y=\mathbb T_k^{n_0}u^{\mathrm s}_k$. For a probability measure, a
nondecreasing and a nonincreasing function have $\int fg\le\int f\int g$,
because $\iint(f(x)-f(x'))(g(x)-g(x'))\le0$; with $f=\lambda^{2n_0}$,
$g=1-\lambda$ and (S1),
\[
\int\lambda^{2n_0}(1-\lambda)\,d\nu\le\kappa a_k\|y\|^2 .
\]
So $y$ satisfies (S1) as well, and Jensen's inequality for $\lambda^{2j}$,
$j=n_1-n_0$, gives $\|\mathbb T_k^jy\|^2\ge\|y\|^2(1-\kappa a_k)^{2j}\ge
\|y\|^2e^{-4\kappa(t_1-t_0)}$, since $\log(1-x)\ge-2x$ on $[0,\frac12]$ and
$a_kj\le t_1-t_0$. Hence
\[
\|W(\mathbb T_k)u^{\mathrm s}_k\|\ge\|w\|_1\|\mathbb T_k^{n_1}u^{\mathrm s}_k\|
\ge\|w\|_1e^{-2\kappa(t_1-t_0)}\|y\| ,
\]
while $\|W(\mathbb T_k)u^{\mathrm h}_k\|\le\|w\|_1\|\mathbb T_k^{n_0}
u^{\mathrm h}_k\|\le\theta\|W(\mathbb T_k)u^{\mathrm s}_k\|$ by (S2). Let
$\chi_k=W(\mathbb T_k)u^{\mathrm s}_k/\|W(\mathbb T_k)u^{\mathrm s}_k\|$. Its
energy is
\[
\frac{\int W^2(1-\lambda)\,d\nu}{\int W^2d\nu}\le
\frac{\|w\|_1^2\kappa a_k\|y\|^2}{\|w\|_1^2e^{-4\kappa(t_1-t_0)}\|y\|^2}
=\kappa a_ke^{4\kappa(t_1-t_0)} ,
\]
and with $r=\|W(\mathbb T_k)u^{\mathrm h}_k\|/\|W(\mathbb T_k)u^{\mathrm s}_k\|
\le\theta$ its overlap with $\Psi_k=\Psi_{A_k}/\|\Psi_{A_k}\|$ is at least
$(1-r)/(1+r)\ge(1-\theta)/(1+\theta)$. Corollary~\ref{cor:trial} gives the
rest.
\end{proof}

Condition (S1) asks for a soft component of the slice field whose first-step
energy is bounded in physical units. Remark~\ref{rem:compositeslice} shows why
the whole slice field is not a candidate: at next order its first-step energy
grows like $\log(\sqrt\tau/a_k)$, through a hard component of norm of order
$g^2$. Condition (S2) asks that this hard component, propagated to the
earliest time $t_0$ of the packet, be a fixed fraction of the soft part.
For the free field of Remark~\ref{rem:freeslice}, (S1) holds with
$u^{\mathrm h}_k=0$: since $(1-e^{-a_k\omega})/a_k\le\omega\le
a_k^{-1}\sinh(a_k\omega)$, the first-step energy
$\ip{u_k}{(1-\mathbb T_k)u_k}/(a_k\|u_k\|^2)$ has a numerator whose
integrand is at most a multiple of $e^{-8t_f|p|^2/\pi^2}$, and a denominator $D_k(0)$ with a positive limit, as
in that remark. For the unflowed magnetic energy the whole slice field
violates (S1) by a factor of order $1/a_k$, and the decomposition has to
separate its hard modes. Both conditions concern energies of order $1/a_k$ at
physical times below $t_0$; they are ultraviolet statements, the subject of a
renormalisation group analysis between the scales $a_k$ and
$t_0$~\cite{Bal84a,Bal85a,Bal89b}, and they are not proved here. By
Corollary~\ref{cor:trial} the trial-state condition is the least hypothesis
that gives $(\mathrm N_u)$, since it is also necessary; by
Proposition~\ref{prop:cubecompatible}(ii) it is not a consequence of the cube
estimate in the form used by Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer}; and (S1)--(S2) are
its form for the slice field.

\begin{remark}\label{rem:independence}
There is no circularity between $(\mathrm N_u)$ and (D). The proofs of
Proposition~\ref{prop:ratioexact}, Lemma~\ref{lem:trial},
Corollary~\ref{cor:trial} and Theorem~\ref{thm:softhard} use reflection
positivity, the transfer matrix and the spectral theorem only, and
Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit}(i)--(iv), including the renormalisation
$Z_k=C_k(0)^{-1/2}$, does not use (D). Conversely, Theorems~\ref{thm:onescale}
and~\ref{thm:transfer} use no lower bound on any correlation. The two
statements are opposite inequalities on the same spectral measures: (D)
bounds $\rho_k$ from above by $e^{-m_*\sigma}$, $(\mathrm N_u)$ from below.
Their joint validity requires only the compatibility relations
$\alpha\ge c^{2\ell/\sigma}$, $m_*\le\sigma^{-1}\log(1/c)$ and
$m_*\le8\kappa/v^2$ of Proposition~\ref{prop:cubecompatible} and
Corollary~\ref{cor:trial}, all of them statements in physical units. Neither
implies the other. Strong-coupling Wilson measures satisfy the hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer} and violate $(\mathrm N_u)$
(Proposition~\ref{prop:cubecompatible}(ii)). In the free abelian theory the
quadratic approximation of the forest gauge violates (D) at every size and
stiffness (Proposition~\ref{prop:gaussforest}), while the zero-momentum packet
of the magnetic energy has slice ratios with positive limits
(Remark~\ref{rem:compositeslice}) and so satisfies $(\mathrm N_0)$ by
Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}(iii); (D) depends on the gauge and
$(\mathrm N_u)$ does not. Theorem~\ref{thm:higher}(iv) derives $(\mathrm N_0)$
from the order-two cases of (T1) and (T2), and
Proposition~\ref{prop:treecritical} shows that those cases fail wherever the
lattice gap is bounded below, in particular in the strong-coupling range,
where the hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer} holds.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Slab energies of local fields}\label{ssec:slabenergy}

This subsection tests the first condition of Corollary~\ref{cor:trial}
against the most direct estimate for it: a pointwise bound on the change of a
gauge-invariant function of one slice in one time step by the time-like
plaquettes, combined with an upper bound on the plaquette energy. Both bounds
are proved with explicit constants. The combination cannot give the
condition along any tuning with $a_k\beta_k\to0$, whatever the trial
function, and the one-loop tuning is of this kind.

Let $G\subset\mathrm U(N)$ be a closed subgroup, let $|M|^2=\Tr(M^*M)$ be the
Frobenius norm, so that $|gMh|=|M|$ for $g,h\in G$, and put $s_p=1-N^{-1}
\ReTr U_p$, so that $|U_p-I|^2=2Ns_p$. Write $U_{(y,\mu)}$ for the link from
$y$ to $y+\hat\mu$, with $\mu=0$ the time direction. Let $\omega$ be a state
as in Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}, with transfer matrix $\mathbb T$, and let
$\varepsilon_\omega=\sup_p\omega(s_p)$, which is $\omega(s_p)$ for every
plaquette $p$ when $\omega$ is invariant under translations and the
hypercubic group. A \emph{slice function} is a function
$X$ of the spatial links of the slice $x_0=0$ that depends only on the links
of a finite set $E$, is invariant under the gauge transformations of that
slice, and satisfies
\[
|X(U)-X(V)|\le\sum_{e\in E}L_e|U_e-V_e|
\]
with constants $L_e\ge0$; put $\Lambda(X)=\sum_{e\in E}L_e$. For a spatial link
$e=((0,x),i)$ let $p_e$ be the time-like plaquette with lower edge $e$, with
holonomy
\[
U_{p_e}=U_{((0,x),i)}U_{((0,x+\hat\imath),0)}U_{((1,x),i)}^{-1}
U_{((0,x),0)}^{-1}.
\]

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:slabdirichlet}
Let $X$ be a bounded slice function.

\textup{(i)} $\ip{\Psi_X}{(1-\mathbb T)\Psi_X}=\frac12\omega(|\tau_1X-X|^2)$,
and both sides are unchanged when a constant is added to $X$.

\textup{(ii)} Pointwise, $|\tau_1X-X|\le\sum_{e\in E}L_e|U_{p_e}-I|$.

\textup{(iii)} $\ip{\Psi_X}{(1-\mathbb T)\Psi_X}\le N\Lambda(X)^2
\varepsilon_\omega$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) The reflection $\vartheta$ in the plane $x_0=0$ fixes the spatial links
of that slice, so $\vartheta(\bar X)=\bar X$ and $\|\Psi_X\|^2=\omega(|X|^2)$.
As in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}, $\Psi_{\tau_1X}=
\mathbb T\Psi_X$, so $\ip{\Psi_X}{\mathbb T\Psi_X}=\omega(\bar X\tau_1X)$, a
real number because $\mathbb T$ is self-adjoint. Translation invariance gives
$\omega(|\tau_1X|^2)=\omega(|X|^2)$, hence
\[
\omega(|\tau_1X-X|^2)=2\omega(|X|^2)-2\omega(\bar X\tau_1X)=
2\ip{\Psi_X}{(1-\mathbb T)\Psi_X} .
\]
The right side of the identity depends on $X$ only through $\tau_1X-X$.

(ii) For a site $x$ of the slice put $g(x)=U_{((0,x),0)}$, and for
$e=((0,x),i)$ put $V_e=g(x)U_{((1,x),i)}g(x+\hat\imath)^{-1}$. The function
$\tau_1X$ is $X$ evaluated on the links $U_{((1,x),i)}$ in place of
$U_{((0,x),i)}$, and gauge invariance under $g$ gives $\tau_1X(U)=X(V)$.
Hence $|\tau_1X-X|\le\sum_eL_e|V_e-U_e|$. Now $|V_e-U_e|=|U_e^{-1}V_e-I|$,
and $U_e^{-1}V_e=U_e^{-1}U_{p_e}^{-1}U_e$, so $|V_e-U_e|=|U_{p_e}^{-1}-I|=
|U_{p_e}-I|$.

(iii) By (i), (ii) and the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality,
\[
\ip{\Psi_X}{(1-\mathbb T)\Psi_X}\le\frac12\Lambda(X)\sum_eL_e\,\omega
(|U_{p_e}-I|^2)\le N\Lambda(X)^2\varepsilon_\omega . \qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

The plaquette energy of \cite[Lemma~382]{BBLR26} carries a factor
$\log\beta_W$. It comes from the lower bound on the partition function alone;
an upper bound in temporal gauge removes it.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:energybeta}
Let $G=\SU(2)$ and let $\mu_L$ be the Wilson measure on the torus
$\Lambda_L=(\Z/L\Z)^4$, with $L\ge2$ and $\beta_W\ge1$. Then
$\varepsilon_L=\E_{\mu_L}s_p$ satisfies
\[
\varepsilon_L\le\frac1{\beta_W}\Bigl(K_1+\frac{K_2}{L^4}+\frac{15}{16}\Bigl(
\frac1L+\frac1{3L^4}\Bigr)\log\beta_W+\frac{K_3}{\beta_W}\Bigr)
\]
with $K_1=3.7148$, $K_2=0.4699$ and $K_3=0.149$. In particular every limit as
$L\to\infty$ of the measures $\mu_L$ along a subsequence, on the local
functions, has $\varepsilon\le(K_1+K_3/\beta_W)/\beta_W$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $\beta=\beta_W$, $Z(\beta)=\int e^{-\beta S}dU$ with $S=\sum_ps_p$, and
$\theta\in[0,\pi]$ for the angle of an element $\cos\theta\,I+\sin\theta\,J$
of $\SU(2)$; it has Haar density $\frac2\pi\sin^2\theta$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:haarsu2}), it is a
bi-invariant distance from $I$, and $\frac12\Tr U=\cos\theta$. The measure
$\mu_L$ is invariant under the translations and the hypercubic group of the
torus, so $\E_{\mu_L}S=6L^4\varepsilon_L$.

\emph{Convexity.} The function $\log Z$ is convex with derivative
$-\E_{\mu_L}S$ at $\beta$, so for $0<\lambda<1$
\[
6L^4(1-\lambda)\beta\,\varepsilon_L\le\log Z(\lambda\beta)-\log Z(\beta) .
\]

\emph{Upper bound.} The integrand is gauge invariant, so the time links
$U_{((t,x),0)}$ with $0\le t\le L-2$, which form a forest, may be set to $I$
without changing $Z$; this is the substitution $U\mapsto U^g$ with $g$ built
along each tree from its root, which preserves the Haar measure. Drop the
spatial plaquettes and the time-like plaquettes between the times $L-1$ and
$0$, whose weights are at most $1$. For each spatial link $(x,i)$ the
remaining weights are $\prod_{t=0}^{L-2}f(V_tV_{t+1}^{-1})$ with
$V_t=U_{((t,x),i)}$ and $f(U)=e^{-\beta(1-\frac12\Tr U)}$; integrating
$V_0,\dots,V_{L-2}$ in turn, each integral is $z(\beta)=\int f\,dU$ by the
invariance of the Haar measure. Hence $Z(\beta)\le z(\beta)^{3L^3(L-1)}$.
With $y=\sin(\theta/2)$,
\begin{align*}
z(\beta)&=\frac2\pi\int_0^\pi e^{-\beta(1-\cos\theta)}\sin^2\theta\,d\theta\\
&=\frac{16}\pi\int_0^1e^{-2\beta y^2}y^2\sqrt{1-y^2}\,dy\le
\sqrt{\frac2\pi}\,\beta^{-3/2},
\end{align*}
by $\sqrt{1-y^2}\le1$ and $\int_0^\infty e^{-2\beta y^2}y^2dy=\frac{\sqrt\pi}4
(2\beta)^{-3/2}$.

\emph{Lower bound.} Fix a spanning tree of $\Lambda_L$ and set its $L^4-1$
links to $I$, again without changing $Z$; $3L^4+1$ links remain. Let $0<\delta
\le\pi/2$ and let $\nu$ be the Haar measure restricted to $\{\theta\le\delta\}$
and normalised. Since $\sin t/t$ decreases on $(0,\pi)$,
\[
\frac2\pi\int_0^\delta\sin^2t\,dt\ge\frac{2\delta^3}{3\pi}\Bigl(\frac{\sin
\delta}\delta\Bigr)^2 .
\]
By Jensen's inequality for the product of the measures $\nu$ on the remaining
links, $\log Z(\beta)\ge(3L^4+1)\log\frac{2\delta^3}{3\pi}(\frac{\sin\delta}
\delta)^2-\beta\sum_p\E_\nu s_p$. The set $\{\theta\le\delta\}$ is invariant
under conjugation and inversion, so $\E_\nu U=mI$ and $\E_\nu U^{-1}=mI$ with
$m=\E_\nu\cos\theta\in[0,1]$. A plaquette has at most four links off the
tree, independent under $\nu$, so $\E_\nu\frac12\Tr U_p=m^j$ with $j\le4$ and
$\E_\nu s_p=1-m^j\le4(1-m)\le2\E_\nu\theta^2$. With $w(t)=\sin^2t/t^2$
nonincreasing and $t^2$ increasing, Chebyshev's inequality for the measure
proportional to $t^2dt$ on $[0,\delta]$ gives $\E_\nu\theta^2=\int_0^\delta
t^2w\,t^2dt/\int_0^\delta w\,t^2dt\le\frac35\delta^2$. Hence $\E_\nu s_p\le
\frac65\delta^2$.

\emph{The constants.} Take $\lambda=\frac15$ and $\delta^2=c/\beta$ with
$c=\frac58$, so that $\delta^2\le\frac58<\pi^2/4$. Dividing by
$6L^4(1-\lambda)\beta$ and collecting the terms in $\log\beta$ gives the
bound, with
\begin{gather*}
K_1=\tfrac54\Bigl(\tfrac14\log\tfrac2\pi-\tfrac34\log\lambda-\tfrac12\log
\tfrac2{3\pi}-\tfrac34\log c+\tfrac65c\Bigr),\\
K_2=\tfrac5{24}\Bigl(-\log\tfrac2{3\pi}-\tfrac32\log c\Bigr),
\end{gather*}
after dropping the term $-\frac5{8L}(\frac12\log\frac2\pi-\frac32\log
\lambda)$, which is negative. The factor $(\sin\delta/\delta)^2$ contributes
at most $\frac54(1+\frac1{48})\frac{y}{1-y}$ with $y=\delta^2/6\le c/(6\beta)$,
by $\sin\delta/\delta\ge1-y$ and $-\log(1-y)\le y/(1-y)$, which is at
most $K_3/\beta$ inside the bracket. With $\lambda=\frac15$ and $c=\frac58$
the logarithms collect to
\begin{gather*}
K_1=\tfrac54\bigl(2\log2+\tfrac12\log3+\tfrac14\log\pi+\tfrac34\bigr),\\
K_2=\tfrac5{24}\bigl(\tfrac72\log2+\log3+\log\pi-\tfrac32\log5\bigr),
\end{gather*}
and $\frac54\cdot\frac{49}{48}\cdot\frac{c/6}{1-c/6}=\frac{1225}{8256}<
0.1484$. The series $2\operatorname{artanh}x=2\sum_{k\ge0}x^{2k+1}/(2k+1)$,
whose tail after any term is at most the next term divided by $1-x^2$,
applied to $\log2=2\operatorname{artanh}\frac13$, $\log\frac32=2\operatorname
{artanh}\frac15$ and $\log\frac54=2\operatorname{artanh}\frac19$, gives
$\log2<0.6931472$, $\log3<1.0986123$ and $\log5>1.6094379$. With $\pi<3.1416$
and $\log(1+x)\le x-\frac{x^2}2+\frac{x^3}3$ at $x=0.0472$, $\log\pi<\log3+
0.046122<1.144735$. Hence $K_1<3.71474$ and $K_2<0.46984$. At fixed $\lambda$ the
value $c=\frac58$ minimises $K_1$. The last claim holds because $s_p$ is a local
function.
\end{proof}

For comparison, \cite[Lemma~382]{BBLR26} gives the bound $(\log\beta_W+
4.9082)/\beta_W$ for $\varepsilon_L$, which is larger at every $\beta_W\ge1$
in the limit $L\to\infty$.
With Lemma~\ref{lem:slabdirichlet}, a slice function at a limit state of
Proposition~\ref{prop:energybeta} has
\[
\ip{\Psi_X}{(1-\mathbb T)\Psi_X}\le\frac{2\Lambda(X)^2}{\beta_W}\Bigl(K_1+
\frac{K_3}{\beta_W}\Bigr),
\]
a bound of order one in lattice units at bounded $\Lambda(X)$. Whether it can
serve as the first condition of Corollary~\ref{cor:trial} depends on the
variance of $X$ alone, and the next proposition shows that it cannot along
the tunings of interest.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:lipobstruction}
Let $X$ be a real bounded slice function with $\Var_\omega X>0$, and let
$\chi=\Psi_{X-\omega(X)}/\|\Psi_{X-\omega(X)}\|$.

\textup{(i)} $\Var_\omega X\le N\Lambda(X)^2$. Hence the bound of
Lemma~\textup{\ref{lem:slabdirichlet}(iii)} for $\ip\chi{(1-\mathbb
T)\chi}$, which is $N\Lambda(X)^2\varepsilon_\omega/\Var_\omega X$, is at least
$\varepsilon_\omega$.

\textup{(ii)} Let $G=\SU(2)$ and let $\omega_k$ be states as in
Appendix~\textup{\ref{app:transfer}} that are Wilson Gibbs states at $\beta_k$
in the sense of Sec.~\textup{\ref{ssec:uniform}}. If that bound
gives the first condition of Corollary~\textup{\ref{cor:trial}} with a
constant $\kappa$, then $(4\beta_k+1)a_k\ge1/\kappa$. Along a tuning with
$a_k\beta_k\to0$, no choice of slice functions $X_k$ gives that condition
through Lemma~\textup{\ref{lem:slabdirichlet}(iii)}.

\textup{(iii)} Let the Lie algebra $\g$ of $G$ have trivial centre. If $X$,
as a function on $G^E$, is differentiable at the configuration with every
link equal to $I$, its differential there vanishes.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) For any two configurations $U$ and $V$,
\[
|X(U)-X(V)|\le\Lambda(X)\max_e|U_e-V_e|\le2\sqrt N\Lambda(X),
\]
since $|U_e|=|V_e|=\sqrt N$. A real random
variable with values in an interval of length $\ell$ has variance at most
$\ell^2/4$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:slabdirichlet}(i), $\ip\chi{(1-\mathbb T)\chi}=
\ip{\Psi_X}{(1-\mathbb T)\Psi_X}/\Var_\omega X$, since $\|\Psi_{X-\omega(X)}
\|^2=\omega(|X-\omega(X)|^2)$.

(ii) By (i) the bound is at least $\varepsilon_{\omega_k}$, which is at least
$1/(4\beta_k+1)$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:plaqvar}. If it is at most $\kappa
a_k$, then $1\le\kappa a_k(4\beta_k+1)$.

(iii) A constant gauge transformation $h$ on the slice gives $X(hUh^{-1})=
X(U)$. For $A=(A_e)\in\g^E$ let $\phi(A)$ be the derivative of
$t\mapsto X((e^{tA_e})_e)$ at $t=0$; $\phi$ is linear, and
$\phi(\mathrm{Ad}_hA)=\phi(A)$ for $h\in G$. Averaging over the Haar measure,
$\phi(A)=\phi(\bar A)$ with $\bar A_e=\int\mathrm{Ad}_hA_e\,dh$. Each $\bar A_e$
is invariant under $\mathrm{Ad}_G$, hence commutes with $\g$, and so vanishes.
The curves $t\mapsto e^{tA_e}$ span the tangent space at $I$.
\end{proof}

The one-loop tuning has $\beta_W=\frac{11}{3\pi^2}\log(1/a)+O(1)$ for $\SU(2)$, and the two-loop tuning adds a term of order $\log\log(1/a)$ \cite[Remark~247]{BBLR26}, so $a_k\beta_k\to0$ along either, and Proposition~\ref{prop:lipobstruction}(ii)
applies. Part (i) shows where the loss lies: the bound of
Lemma~\ref{lem:slabdirichlet}(iii) is first order in the time-like plaquettes,
and it gives an energy at most $\kappa a$ only if the variance of $X$ is at
least the fraction $\varepsilon_\omega/(\kappa a)$ of its largest possible
value $N\Lambda(X)^2$. Part (iii) shows that a gauge-invariant function has no
first-order term at the configuration with all links $I$. If $X$ is
continuously differentiable, its local Lipschitz constants at configurations
within distance $r$ of that configuration, or of a gauge transform of it, tend
to $0$ with $r$, while the bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:slabdirichlet}(ii) uses the
global constants $L_e$.

The bound does give the trial-state condition in the opposite regime, at the
cost of a lower bound on a variance and on one correlation, which are the
infrared content of $(\mathrm N_u)$.

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:lipreduction}
Let $G=\SU(2)$, let $\omega_k$ be states as in
Appendix~\textup{\ref{app:transfer}} that are limits as in
Proposition~\textup{\ref{prop:energybeta}} at $\beta_k\ge1$, and let $X_k$ be real
slice functions with $X_k^c=X_k-\omega_k(X_k)$. Suppose that there are
$\nu,v>0$ such that for all large $k$
\begin{gather*}
\Var_{\omega_k}X_k\ge2\nu\Lambda(X_k)^2,\\
\omega_k\bigl(X_k^c\,\tau_{u/a_k}A_k\bigr)^2\ge v^2\,\Var_{\omega_k}X_k\,
C_k(2u) .
\end{gather*}
If moreover $a_k\beta_k\ge\kappa_0>0$ for all large $k$, then $(\mathrm N_u)$
holds, and $\rho_k(u,\sigma)\ge(v-\sqrt{\kappa\sigma/2})_+^2$ for
$\sigma\ge2a_k$, with $\kappa=(K_1+K_3)/(\nu\kappa_0)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The vector $\chi_k=\Psi_{X_k^c}/\|\Psi_{X_k^c}\|$ is a unit vector with
$\|\Psi_{X_k^c}\|^2=\Var_{\omega_k}X_k$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:slabdirichlet} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:energybeta}, since $\beta_k\ge1$ and $a_k\beta_k\ge\kappa_0$,
\[
\ip{\chi_k}{(1-\mathbb T_k)\chi_k}\le\frac{2\Lambda(X_k)^2(K_1+K_3)}
{\beta_k\Var_{\omega_k}X_k}\le\frac{K_1+K_3}{\nu\beta_k}\le\kappa a_k .
\]
Since $X_k$ is a real function of the slice $x_0=0$, $\vartheta(X_k^c)=X_k^c$
and $\ip{\chi_k}{\Psi_{k,u}}=\omega_k(X_k^c\tau_{u/a_k}A_k)/(\Var_{\omega_k}
X_k\,C_k(2u))^{1/2}$, whose modulus is at least $v$. Corollary~\ref{cor:trial}
gives the rest.
\end{proof}

The hypotheses of Corollary~\ref{cor:lipreduction} are not proved here. The
first asks that $X_k$ take values spread over a fixed fraction of the range
allowed by its Lipschitz constants, at couplings where the plaquettes are of
size $\beta_k^{-1/2}$; the second is a lower bound on one correlation at
physical distance. Together with Proposition~\ref{prop:lipobstruction}, the
corollary locates the estimate: the upper bound on the slab energy that
reflection positivity and the plaquette energy provide is of order
$\beta_k^{-1}$ in lattice units, it meets the scale $a_k$ only where
$a_k\beta_k$ is bounded below, and even there it transfers the whole of
$(\mathrm N_u)$ to lower bounds on a variance and on an overlap.

\subsection{The axioms for the flowed fields}\label{ssec:osaxioms}

Statement (E) asks for more than the nontriviality of
Sec.~\ref{ssec:ratiocube}. This subsection determines, for the spatially
flowed fields of Sec.~\ref{ssec:higher}, which of the Osterwalder--Schrader
axioms follow from the hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer} once the
higher renormalised correlations are bounded, and which further statements
are needed. Three are needed; together they suffice, and each is necessary:
a uniform moment bound (M), which contains $(\mathrm N_0)$; invariance under
one spatial rotation of infinite order (W); and coincidence of the
subsequential limits (U). The spatial flow leaves at most the symmetry
$\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$ of space and time reflection, and Gaussian
examples show that neither (W) nor (U) follows from the gap, reflection
positivity, the lattice symmetries and the tree bounds.

The notation is that of Sec.~\ref{ssec:higher}: $\Phi$ is a bounded real
gauge-invariant function of finitely many spatial links of the slice
$\{x_0=0\}$, continuous in them, $\varphi_k(x)=\Phi_x\circ\Phi_{s_k}$ with
$s_k=\tau/a_k^2$, $A_k(f)$ is the centred packet of $f\in C_c(\R^4)$, $f_0$ is
a fixed nonzero function supported in $\{t>0\}$, $C_k(0)>0$ and
$Z_k=C_k(0)^{-1/2}$. Let $W_3$ be the group of the $48$ signed permutations of
the spatial coordinates, $\theta(t,\mathbf x)=(-t,\mathbf x)$, and $\Gamma$ the
group of order $96$ generated by $W_3$ and $\theta$, the subgroup of the
hyperoctahedral group $B_4$ that preserves the time axis. We assume that
$\Phi$ is invariant under the action of $W_3$ on the links of the slice that
fixes the site $0$. Averaging $\Phi$ over $W_3$ achieves this; the sum of
$N-\ReTr U_p$ over the twelve spatial plaquettes $p$ with a corner at $0$ is
an example.

Throughout, the states $\omega_k$ at $\beta_k$ satisfy the hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer}: $D(n_k,\alpha)$ holds with $n_ka_k\le\ell$. By
Theorem~\ref{thm:onescale} and Proposition~\ref{prop:state}, each
$\omega_k$ is the restriction of the unique Gibbs measure of $\gamma^F$, is
the free-boundary limit along every exhaustion of $\Z^4$, is invariant under
translations and the hypercubic group and is reflection positive, and its
transfer matrix has gap at least $\log(1/\alpha)/(2n_k)\ge m_*a_k$ on
$\Omega_k^\perp$, with $m_*=\log(1/\alpha)/(2\ell)$. The infinite-volume
state is therefore unique at each $k$, and the only non-uniqueness left is
in the limit $k\to\infty$. The spatial flow commutes with $\Gamma$
(Proposition~\ref{prop:spatialflow}(i) and the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(ii)) but not with the elements of $B_4$ that
exchange time with a spatial axis, so $\Gamma$, not $B_4$, is the lattice
symmetry of the flowed packets.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(M)},leftmargin=*]
\item \emph{Moment bound.} For every $n\ge1$ and every cube $Q\subset\R^4$
there are $M_n(Q)<\infty$ and $k_0$ such that
\[
\Bigl|\omega_k\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^nZ_kA_k(f_j)\Bigr)\Bigr|\le
M_n(Q)\prod_{j=1}^n\|f_j\|_\infty
\]
for all $k\ge k_0$ and all $f_j\in C_c(\R^4)$ supported in $Q$.
\end{enumerate}

By Theorem~\ref{thm:higher}(i), (T1)--(T3) imply (M). The case $n=2$ of (M)
is not the bound of Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit}(i), which concerns reflected
pairs of translates of $f_0$ and holds by reflection positivity alone; (M)
concerns all test functions in $Q$, including those closer to the
reflection plane than $f_0$, and it implies $(\mathrm N_0)$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(v)). By Remark~\ref{rem:markov}, no bound on the
two-point functions gives (M) for $n=4$.

A \emph{polynomial} is a finite formal sum $P=\sum_\beta c_\beta
\prod_{j=1}^{n_\beta}f_{\beta,j}$ with $c_\beta\in\C$ and $f_{\beta,j}\in
C_c(\R^4)$; its lattice version is $P_k=\sum_\beta c_\beta\prod_jZ_k
A_k(f_{\beta,j})$, and for a family $S=(S_n)$ with $S_0=1$ we put
$S(P)=\sum_\beta c_\beta S_{n_\beta}(f_{\beta,1},\dots,f_{\beta,n_\beta})$.
The polynomials $\bar P$, $\theta P$, $\tau_yP$ and $gP$ are obtained by
replacing every $f_{\beta,j}$ by $\overline{f_{\beta,j}}$,
$f_{\beta,j}\circ\theta$, $f_{\beta,j}(\cdot-y)$ and $f_{\beta,j}\circ
g^{-1}$, products of polynomials are formal products, and $P$ is \emph{at
positive times} if every $f_{\beta,j}$ is supported in $\{t>0\}$. We write
$\Psi(f_0)$ for $\Psi(P)$ with $P=f_0$.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:osaxioms}
Assume the hypothesis of Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:transfer}} and
\textup{(M)}, with $\Phi$ real and $W_3$-invariant. Every sequence of $k$
has a subsequence along which the following hold.

\textup{(i)} For all $n$ and all $f_j\in C_c(\R^4)$ the limit
\[
S_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)=\lim_k\omega_k\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^nZ_kA_k(f_j)\Bigr)
\]
exists. Each $S_n$ is symmetric and multilinear, bounded by
$M_n(Q)\prod_j\|f_j\|_\infty$ on functions supported in a cube $Q$, and a
distribution on $(\R^4)^n$; $S_1=0$.

\textup{(ii)} Each $S_n$ is invariant under the translations of $\R^4$ and
under $\Gamma$: $S_n(f_1\circ g^{-1},\dots,f_n\circ g^{-1})=S_n(f_1,\dots,
f_n)$ for $g\in\Gamma$. Moreover $S(\bar P)=\overline{S(P)}$.

\textup{(iii)} $S(\theta\bar P\cdot P)\ge0$ for every polynomial $P$ at
positive times. The completion of the quotient of these polynomials by the
null space of this form is a Hilbert space $\mathcal H$ with vectors
$\Psi(P)$, $\ip{\Psi(P)}{\Psi(P')}=S(\theta\bar P\cdot P')$, and
$\Omega=\Psi(1)$.

\textup{(iv)} There are a self-adjoint $H\ge0$ with $\Psi(\tau_{(t,0)}P)=
e^{-tH}\Psi(P)$ for $t\ge0$, a strongly continuous unitary representation
$U$ of $\R^3$ with $\Psi(\tau_{(0,x)}P)=U(x)\Psi(P)$, and a unitary
representation $V$ of $W_3$ with $\Psi(gP)=V(g)\Psi(P)$. They commute with
$H$, $V(g)U(x)V(g)^{-1}=U(gx)$, $H\Omega=0$, and $U$ and $V$ fix $\Omega$.

\textup{(v)} $\Psi(f_0)$ is a unit vector orthogonal to $\Omega$, there is a
dyadic $\sigma>0$ with $C_k(\sigma)\ge\frac12C_k(0)$ for all large $k$, and
$(\mathrm N_0)$ holds.

\textup{(vi)} $\sigma(H)\subset\{0\}\cup[m_*,\infty)$ and $\ker H=\C\Omega$.
For polynomials $P,P'$ at positive times and $t\ge0$,
\[
\begin{split}
&\bigl|S(\theta\bar P\cdot\tau_{(t,0)}P')-\overline{S(P)}\,S(P')\bigr|\\
&\qquad\le e^{-m_*t}\,\|\Psi(P)-S(P)\Omega\|\,\|\Psi(P')-S(P')\Omega\| .
\end{split}
\]
The joint spectral measure of $H$ and the generators of $U$ is carried by
$\{(0,0)\}\cup\bigl([m_*,\infty)\times\R^3\bigr)$.

\textup{(vii)} If $M_n(Q)\le C_n(1+L)^{p_n}$ for every cube $Q$ of side $L$
centred at $0$, each $S_n$ extends to a tempered distribution on
$\R^{4n}$.

Conversely, if for every $n$ and all $f_j\in C_c(\R^4)$ the sequence
$\omega_k(\prod_jZ_kA_k(f_j))$ is bounded, then \textup{(M)} holds.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(i) The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:higher}(ii) uses part (i) of that theorem
only through the bound $M_n(Q)$, and applies verbatim. $S_1=0$ because the
packets are centred.

(ii) Translation invariance is proved as in Theorem~\ref{thm:higher}(ii). Let
$g\in\Gamma$; it maps $\Z^4$ to itself and fixes the site $0$. It acts on
configurations by moving the links, a link whose orientation is reversed
carrying the inverse group element. Inversion is an isometry of the
bi-invariant metric, and $g$ preserves the Wilson action of every time
slice, so this action maps solutions of the flow equation to solutions and
commutes with $\Phi_s$; for $g=\theta$ this is
Proposition~\ref{prop:spatialflow}(i). Write $gF$ for the function $U\mapsto
F(g^{-1}U)$. Since $\Phi$ is $W_3$-invariant and $\theta$ fixes the spatial
links of the slice $\{x_0=0\}$, $g\Phi_x=\Phi_{gx}$, hence
$g\varphi_k(x)=\varphi_k(gx)$, and substituting $y=g^{-1}x$,
\[
A_k(f\circ g^{-1})=\sum_ya_k^4f(a_ky)\bigl(\varphi_k(gy)-c_k\bigr)
=g\,A_k(f),
\]
where $c_k=\omega_k(\varphi_k(x))$ does not depend on $x$. The state
$\omega_k$ is invariant under the hypercubic group, so $\omega_k(\prod_j
A_k(f_j\circ g^{-1}))=\omega_k(\prod_jA_k(f_j))$, and the limits agree.
Since $\Phi$ is real, $A_k(\bar f)=\overline{A_k(f)}$, which gives the last
claim.

(iii) This is the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:higher}(iii), which uses only
the bound of (i).

(iv) Let $P$ be at positive times and $t\ge0$ dyadic. For large $k$,
$t/a_k\in\Z$, and translation invariance of $\omega_k$ and of the flow gives
$(\tau_{(t,0)}P)_k=\tau_{t/a_k}P_k$. In the reconstruction of
Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}, $\Psi_{\tau_nF}=\mathbb T_k^n\Psi_F$ (proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit}), so
\[
\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(\bar P_k)\tau_{2t/a_k}P_k\bigr)
=\bigl\|\mathbb T_k^{t/a_k}\Psi_{P_k}\bigr\|^2\le
\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(\bar P_k)P_k\bigr).
\]
By (i) and the approximation of $\theta$ by the lattice reflection in the
proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:higher}(iii), the left side tends to
$S(\theta\bar P\cdot\tau_{(2t,0)}P)$, which equals
$\|\Psi(\tau_{(t,0)}P)\|^2$ by translation invariance and
$\theta\tau_{(t,0)}=\tau_{(-t,0)}\theta$, and the right side tends to
$\|\Psi(P)\|^2$. The bound of (i) makes $S(\theta\bar P'\cdot P')$
continuous under uniform convergence of the test functions in a fixed cube,
so $\|\Psi(\tau_{(t,0)}P)\|\le\|\Psi(P)\|$ for all $t\ge0$, and
$\|\Psi(\tau_{(t,0)}P)-\Psi(P)\|\to0$ as $t\to0$, each monomial of
$\tau_{(t,0)}P-P$ being a telescoping sum of monomials with one factor
$f(\cdot-(t,0))-f$. Hence $\tau_{(t,0)}$ maps null vectors to null vectors
and induces contractions $\mathsf T(t)$ of $\mathcal H$ with
$\mathsf T(s)\mathsf T(t)=\mathsf T(s+t)$, symmetric by translation
invariance and $\theta\tau_{(t,0)}=\tau_{(-t,0)}\theta$, and strongly
continuous on a dense set and hence everywhere. A strongly continuous
semigroup of symmetric contractions is $e^{-tH}$ with $H\ge0$
self-adjoint~\cite{OS73}. The spatial translations and the elements of
$W_3$ preserve the positive times, commute with $\theta$ and preserve the
form by (ii); they have inverses of the same kind, and so induce unitaries,
strongly continuous in $x$ by the argument just given. The relations follow
from those of the maps of $\R^4$, and $\tau_y1=g1=1$.

(v) As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:higher}(iv),
$C_k(t)-C_k(0)=\omega_k(A_k(\bar f_0\circ\theta_k)A_k(f_{0,t}-f_0))$ for
dyadic $t$ and large $k$, with $f_{0,t}=f_0(\cdot-(t,0))$ and $\theta_k$ the
lattice reflection. With $Q$ a cube containing the supports of
$\bar f_0\circ\theta_k$ and of $f_{0,t}$ for all $k$ and $t\le1$, (M) for
$n=2$ gives $Z_k^2|C_k(t)-C_k(0)|\le M_2(Q)\|f_0\|_\infty
\|f_{0,t}-f_0\|_\infty$, which is at most $\frac12$ for a small dyadic $t$
by uniform continuity. So $C_k(\sigma)/C_k(0)\ge\frac12$ with $\sigma=t$,
which is $(\mathrm N_0)$. Further $\|\Psi(f_0)\|^2=S_2(\bar f_0\circ\theta,
f_0)=\lim_kZ_k^2C_k(0)=1$ by the same approximation of $\theta$, and
$\ip\Omega{\Psi(f_0)}=S_1(f_0)=0$.

(vi) The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:higher}(vi) uses part (ii) of that
theorem only through the convergence of the moments, which (i) provides. It
gives $\ip v{e^{-tH}v}\le e^{-m_*t}\|v\|^2$ for $v=\Psi(P)-\ip\Omega
{\Psi(P)}\Omega$, $P$ at positive times. These vectors are dense in
$\Omega^\perp$, which $e^{-tH}$ leaves invariant, so the restriction of the
positive operator $e^{-tH}$ to $\Omega^\perp$ has norm at most
$e^{-m_*t}$; the spectral theorem gives the claims on $\sigma(H)$ and
$\ker H$. Here $\ip\Omega{\Psi(P)}=S(P)$ and $\ip{\Psi(P)}\Omega=
S(\theta\bar P)=\overline{S(P)}$ by (ii), and $\ip{\Psi(P)}{e^{-tH}
\Psi(P')}-\ip{\Psi(P)}\Omega\ip\Omega{\Psi(P')}=\ip v{e^{-tH}v'}$ with $v,v'$
the projections on $\Omega^\perp$, which gives the inequality. Since $U$
commutes with $H$ and fixes $\Omega$, both reduce $\Omega^\perp$; the joint
spectral measure at a vector of $\Omega^\perp$ is carried by
$[m_*,\infty)\times\R^3$, and at $\Omega$ it is the point mass at $(0,0)$.

(vii) Let $\chi(x)=\prod_\mu(1-|x_\mu|)_+$, so that $\sum_{m\in\Z^4}
\chi(x-m)=1$, and put $\chi_m=\chi(\cdot-m)$, $r=p_n+5$ and
\[
N_r(f)=\sup_x(1+|x|_\infty)^r|f(x)| .
\]
On the support of $\chi_m$, $1+|x|_\infty\ge
\max(1,|m|_\infty)\ge(2+|m|_\infty)/3$, so $\|\chi_mf\|_\infty\le3^r
(2+|m|_\infty)^{-r}N_r(f)$. For $f_j\in C_c(\R^4)$, multilinearity gives
the finite sum
\[
S_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)=\sum_{m_1,\dots,m_n}S_n(\chi_{m_1}f_1,\dots,
\chi_{m_n}f_n),
\]
and the term of $(m_j)$ has its supports in
the cube of side $L=2+2\max_j|m_j|_\infty$, so it is at most
\[
C_n\,2^{p_n}\prod_{j=1}^n(2+|m_j|_\infty)^{p_n}\,\|\chi_{m_j}f_j\|_\infty .
\]
Summing, $|S_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)|\le C_n2^{p_n}(3^rs)^n\prod_jN_r(f_j)$ with
$s=\sum_{m\in\Z^4}(2+|m|_\infty)^{-5}<\infty$. Functions in $C_c(\R^4)$ are
dense in $\mathcal S(\R^4)$ for $N_r$, so $S_n$ extends to a jointly
continuous multilinear form on $\mathcal S(\R^4)^n$, and the kernel
theorem~\cite[Sect.~V.3]{RS80} gives a tempered distribution on $\R^{4n}$.

For the converse, let $C_Q$ be the Banach space of continuous functions
supported in $Q$ with the supremum norm. For fixed $k$ the form
$B_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)=\omega_k(\prod_jZ_kA_k(f_j))$ is continuous on $C_Q^n$,
since $|A_k(f)|\le2\|\Phi\|_\infty a_k^4\#\{x:a_kx\in Q\}\|f\|_\infty$. A
family of continuous $n$-linear forms on a Banach space that is bounded at
every point is bounded in norm: for $n=1$ this is the uniform boundedness
principle~\cite{RS80}, and for $n>1$, by induction, $\sup_k\|B_k(f_1,\cdot)
\|<\infty$ for each $f_1$, so the linear maps $f_1\mapsto B_k(f_1,\cdot)$
into the Banach space of $(n-1)$-linear forms are bounded at every point,
and hence in norm. The bound is $M_n(Q)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:dyadicmoments}
If (M) is replaced by bounds on the dyadic packets only, namely
$\sup_kZ_k^{n+n'}|\omega_k(\vartheta(\prod_{i\le n}\bar A^{y_i}_k)
\prod_{j\le n'}A^{y'_j}_k)|<\infty$ for all $y_i,y'_j\in\mathcal D$, the
construction of Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit}(iii) applies to polynomials in
the vectors $\delta_y$: every monomial is $\mathsf T(s)$ of a monomial at
positive times for a small dyadic $s>0$, so the kernels of the positive
contractions $\mathsf T(\sigma)$ are trivial, and $H=-\log\mathsf T(1)$ is
self-adjoint and nonnegative, with unitary dyadic spatial translations; the
proof of part (vi) gives the gap $m_*$. The limit is nontrivial when
$(\mathrm N_u)$ holds for some $u$, but not only then: when $\rho\equiv0$,
the vectors $\delta_y$ vanish while products of them need not. Without
continuity in the test functions, neither the distributions $S_n$ nor
continuous translations follow.
\end{remark}

Under the tree bounds, the moment bound holds with polynomial constants,
and the limit has two properties that (M) does not give: temperedness with
an $L^1$ bound, and clustering in every direction. For $f\in\mathcal
S(\R^4)$ put $|f|_{(1)}=\max(\|f\|_1,\|f\|_\infty)$; for a tree $T$ on
$\{1,\dots,n\}$, $\zeta\in(\R^4)^{E(T)}$ and $j\ge2$, let $\zeta^T_j$ be the
sum of $\zeta_e$ over the edges of the path from $1$ to $j$ in $T$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:hermite}
Let $\mathsf S$ be a multilinear form on $\mathcal S(\R^4)^n$ with
$|\mathsf S(f_1,\dots,f_n)|\le\sigma\prod_j|f_j|_{(1)}$. Then $\mathsf S$ is
given by a unique tempered distribution on $\R^{4n}$, and for $F\in\mathcal
S(\R^{4n})$, with $L_i=-\partial_i^2+x_i^2+4$ in the $4n$ coordinates,
\[
|\mathsf S(F)|\le\sigma\,\|L_1^3\cdots L_{4n}^3F\|_{L^2(\R^{4n})}
\le\sigma\,236^{4n}(\pi/2)^{2n}|F|_{32n},
\]
where $|F|_m=\sup_{x,\,|\gamma|\le m}(1+|x|^2)^{m/2}|\partial^\gamma F(x)|$
are the norms of the linear growth condition of~\cite{OS75}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The form is jointly continuous, so the kernel
theorem~\cite[Sect.~V.3]{RS80} gives the distribution. Let $h_k$, $k\ge0$, be
the Hermite functions on $\R$, an orthonormal basis of $L^2(\R)$ of real
functions with $(-\partial^2+x^2)h_k=(2k+1)h_k$ and
$xh_k=\sqrt{k/2}\,h_{k-1}+\sqrt{(k+1)/2}\,h_{k+1}$, whose tensor products
$h_\kappa$, $\kappa\in\mathbb N^{4n}$, give an expansion of every
$F\in\mathcal S(\R^{4n})$ that converges in $\mathcal S$~\cite[Sect.~V.3]{RS80}.
For real $g\in\mathcal S(\R)$, $g(x)^2=\int_{-\infty}^x2gg'=-\int_x^\infty
2gg'$, so $\|g\|_\infty^2\le\|g\|_2\|g'\|_2$; with $\|h_k'\|_2^2\le2k+1$
this gives $\|h_k\|_\infty\le(2k+1)^{1/4}$. Applying the recursion twice,
$x^2h_k=\frac12\bigl(\sqrt{k(k-1)}\,h_{k-2}+(2k+1)h_k+\sqrt{(k+1)(k+2)}\,
h_{k+2}\bigr)$, so $\|(1+x^2)h_k\|_\infty\le(2k+\frac52)(2k+5)^{1/4}\le
(2k+5)^{5/4}$. Since $\int_\R(1+x^2)^{-1}dx=\pi$, a function $f$ on $\R^4$
has $|f|_{(1)}\le\pi^4\|\prod_{i=1}^4(1+x_i^2)f\|_\infty$, and for
$\alpha\in\mathbb N^4$ therefore $|h_\alpha|_{(1)}\le\pi^4\prod_i
(2\alpha_i+5)^{5/4}$. The operator $L=\prod_iL_i^3$ is symmetric with
$Lh_\kappa=\prod_i(2\kappa_i+5)^3h_\kappa$, so the coefficient
$c_\kappa=\ip{h_\kappa}F$ has $|c_\kappa|\le\prod_i(2\kappa_i+5)^{-3}
\|LF\|_2$. Hence
\[
|\mathsf S(F)|\le\sum_\kappa|c_\kappa||\mathsf S(h_\kappa)|
\le\sigma\|LF\|_2\Bigl(\pi\sum_{k\ge0}(2k+5)^{-7/4}\Bigr)^{4n},
\]
and $\sum_k(2k+5)^{-7/4}\le5^{-7/4}+\frac23 5^{-3/4}<0.2592$, so the bracket
is below $0.815$. In one variable $L_i^3=\sum c_{ab}x^a\partial^b$ with
$a,b\le6$ and integer coefficients, and expanding and normal ordering gives $\sum|c_{ab}|=236$. Expanding the product,
$\|LF\|_2\le236^{4n}\max\|x^a\partial^bF\|_2$ over multi-indices with
entries at most $6$, and since $\int_{\R^{4n}}\prod_i(1+x_i^2)^{-2}dx=
(\pi/2)^{4n}$, $\|g\|_2\le(\pi/2)^{2n}\|\prod_i(1+x_i^2)g\|_\infty$. Finally
$\prod_i(1+x_i^2)|x_i|^{a_i}\le\prod_i(1+x_i^2)^4\le(1+|x|^2)^{16n}$ and
$|b|\le24n$, so $\|\prod_i(1+x_i^2)x^a\partial^bF\|_\infty\le|F|_{32n}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:ostree}
Let the $\omega_k$ be translation invariant and assume \textup{(T1)--(T3)}.
\textup{(i)} \textup{(M)} holds, with $M_n(Q)\le C_n(1+L)^{2n}$ for cubes of
side $L$ centred at $0$.

\textup{(ii)} Along every subsequence on which the limits $S_n$ of
Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:osaxioms}(i)} exist there are a further
subsequence, a number $b\in[0,B_*]$ and a positive measure $\nu$ on $\R^4$
with $\nu(\R^4)\le1$ such that, for $n\ge2$ and $f_j\in C_c(\R^4)$, the
truncated functions $S^T_n$ of the limit satisfy
\[
\begin{split}
|S^T_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)|\le{}&K_nc^{-n/2}b^{n/2-1}\sum_T\int|f_1(x)|\\
&\times\int\prod_{j=2}^n|f_j(x+\zeta^T_j)|\,d\nu^{\otimes E(T)}(\zeta)\,dx ,
\end{split}
\]
with $b^0=1$.

\textup{(iii)} For $n\ge2$ and every $i$,
\[
|S^T_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)|\le K'_n\|f_i\|_1\prod_{j\ne i}\|f_j\|_\infty
\]
with $K'_n=K_nn^{n-2}c^{-n/2}B_*^{n/2-1}$. Hence $S_n$ extends to
$\mathcal S(\R^4)^n$ and
\[
|S_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)|\le\sigma_n\prod_j|f_j|_{(1)},
\]
where $\sigma_n$ is the
sum over the partitions of $\{1,\dots,n\}$ into blocks of size at least two
of $\prod_BK'_{|B|}$, and Lemma~\textup{\ref{lem:hermite}} applies. If
$K_n\le K^n(n!)^\beta$ for all $n$, then $\sigma_n\le C^n(n!)^{\beta+2}$
with $C=Ke\,c^{-1/2}\max(1,B_*)^{1/2}$, and the Schwinger functions satisfy
the linear growth condition of~\cite{OS75}.

\textup{(iv)} Let $\{1,\dots,n\}=I\cup J$ with $I,J$ disjoint and nonempty,
$y\in\R^4\setminus\{0\}$, and $f^\lambda_j=f_j$ for $j\in I$,
$f^\lambda_j=f_j(\cdot-\lambda y)$ for $j\in J$. Then
\[
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}S_n(f^\lambda_1,\dots,f^\lambda_n)=
S_{|I|}\bigl((f_j)_{j\in I}\bigr)\,S_{|J|}\bigl((f_j)_{j\in J}\bigr).
\]
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:osaxioms}}, $U(\lambda x)$
converges weakly to the projection on $\C\Omega$ as $\lambda\to\infty$ for
every $x\in\R^3\setminus\{0\}$, and $\Omega$ is the only vector invariant
under $U$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(i) This is Theorem~\ref{thm:higher}(i): a moment is a sum over partitions
into blocks of size at least two, each contributing a factor
$|Q|_k\le(L+a_0)^4$, and there are at most $n/2$ blocks.

(ii) By (T1) and (T2), as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:treesum}, the
truncated function of the renormalised packets at level $k$ is at most
$Z_k^nK_nr_k^n\sum_T a_k^{4n}\sum_x\prod_j|f_j(a_kx_j)|\prod_{\{i,j\}\in T}
w_k(x_i-x_j)$. For fixed $T$ change variables to $x_1$ and
$z_e=x_{\mathrm{child}}-x_{\mathrm{parent}}$, the tree being rooted at $1$;
since $w_k$ is even, the sum is $(a_k^4W_k)^{n-1}\int h_k\,d\nu_k^{\otimes
E(T)}$, where $\nu_k=W_k^{-1}\sum_zw_k(z)\delta_{a_kz}$ is a probability
measure ($W_k>0$, since $C_k(0)>0$) and
\[
h_k(\zeta)=a_k^4\sum_{x\in\Z^4}|f_1(a_kx)|\prod_{j\ge2}|f_j(a_kx+\zeta^T_j)| .
\]
With $Z_k^nr_k^n\le(c\,a_k^4W_k)^{-n/2}$ the bound becomes
\[
K_nc^{-n/2}(a_k^4W_k)^{n/2-1}\sum_T\int h_k\,d\nu_k^{\otimes E(T)} .
\]
Choose a
further subsequence along which $a_k^4W_k\to b$ and $\nu_k\to\nu$ vaguely;
then $\nu_k^{\otimes m}\to\nu^{\otimes m}$ vaguely, the masses being at most
one. Let $h(\zeta)=\int|f_1(x)|\prod_{j\ge2}|f_j(x+\zeta^T_j)|\,dx$. It is
continuous, and it has compact support, because $x$ and every
$x+\zeta^T_j$ lie in compact sets when the integrand is nonzero, and
$\zeta_e$ is the difference of two of these points. For $\zeta$ in
$(a_k\Z^4)^{E(T)}$, $h_k(\zeta)$ is a Riemann sum for $h(\zeta)$ of a
family of functions with supports in one compact set and a common modulus
of continuity, so $\sup_\zeta|h_k(\zeta)-h(\zeta)|\to0$. Hence $\int
h_kd\nu_k^{\otimes E(T)}\to\int h\,d\nu^{\otimes E(T)}$. The truncated
functions at level $k$ are polynomials in finitely many moments, which
converge, so the left side converges to $|S^T_n|$.

(iii) Relabel so that $i=1$; the integral in (ii) is at most
$\|f_1\|_1\prod_{j\ge2}\|f_j\|_\infty$, there are $n^{n-2}$ trees, and
$b^{n/2-1}\le B_*^{n/2-1}$. The moments are sums over partitions of products
of truncated functions, which gives $\sigma_n$ on $C_c(\R^4)$; the bound
extends to $\mathcal S(\R^4)$, the closure of $C_c(\R^4)$ for
$|\cdot|_{(1)}$ containing it. If $K_m\le K^m(m!)^\beta$, then, by
$m^{m-2}\le e^mm!$, $K'_m\le C^m(m!)^{\beta+1}$; since $\prod_B|B|!\le n!$
and there are at most $n!$ partitions, $\sigma_n\le C^n(n!)^{\beta+2}$.
With Lemma~\ref{lem:hermite}, $|S_n(F)|\le C^n(n!)^{\beta+2}236^{4n}
(\pi/2)^{2n}|F|_{32n}$, which is the condition of~\cite{OS75} with $s=32$.

(iv) Assume $1\in I$, by symmetry of $S_n$. In a partition of
$\{1,\dots,n\}$ the blocks contained in $I$ or in $J$ do not change their
truncated functions as $\lambda$ varies, by translation invariance. For a
block $B$ meeting both, apply (ii) to $(f^\lambda_j)_{j\in B}$ with a root
in $B\cap I$: the integrand is bounded by $|f_{\mathrm{root}}(x)|\prod
\|f_j\|_\infty$, integrable for $dx\otimes\nu^{\otimes E(T)}$, and tends to
$0$ pointwise as $\lambda\to\infty$, since for $j\in B\cap J$ the point
$x+\zeta^T_j$ is fixed while the support of $f^\lambda_j$ leaves every
compact set. By dominated convergence the truncated function of $B$ tends
to $0$, and the moment tends to the sum over the partitions into blocks in
$I$ and blocks in $J$, which is the product. For the last statement, let
$P,P'$ be at positive times; $\ip{\Psi(P)}{U(\lambda x)\Psi(P')}=S(\theta
\bar P\cdot\tau_{(0,\lambda x)}P')$ tends to $S(\theta\bar P)S(P')=
\ip{\Psi(P)}\Omega\ip\Omega{\Psi(P')}$ by what was just proved, applied to
each monomial. The operators are uniformly bounded and the vectors $\Psi(P)$
are dense, which gives weak convergence; a vector $v$ with $U(x)v=v$ for all
$x$ then has $\|v\|^2=|\ip\Omega v|^2$, so $v\in\C\Omega$.
\end{proof}

Part (iv) is the cluster property of the axioms in every direction, for all
$n$. The gap of Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(vi) gives it with the rate $m_*$
in the time direction only. Spatial clustering does not follow from that
gap and (M) without the tree bounds: the field of
Proposition~\ref{prop:osobstruction}(b) below has the gap, satisfies (M),
and does not cluster in space. With the hypercubic group in place of
$\Gamma$ the exchange of time with a spatial axis would carry the time
clustering to space; the spatial flow removes that exchange.

\emph{Rotations.} For a family $S=(S_n)$ as in
Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(i)--(ii) let
\[
G_S=\bigl\{R\in\mathrm O(4):S_n(f_1\circ R^{-1},\dots,f_n\circ R^{-1})
=S_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)\bigr\},
\]
the condition holding for all $n$ and $f_j\in C_c(\R^4)$. Let $e_0$ be the
unit time vector, $\SO(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}$ the rotations that fix it and
$\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$ the orthogonal maps that preserve the line
$\R e_0$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:rotations}
\textup{(i)} $G_S$ is a closed subgroup of $\mathrm O(4)$ that contains
$\Gamma$, and its identity component is $\{1\}$, $\SO(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}$ or
$\SO(4)$.

\textup{(ii)} $G_S\supseteq\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$ if and only if
$G_S$ contains a rotation of the spatial coordinates of infinite order.

\textup{(iii)} $G_S=\mathrm O(4)$ if and only if $G_S$ contains a rotation
$R$ of infinite order with $Re_0\notin\{\pm e_0\}$, and also if and only if
$G_S$ is infinite and contains some $g$ with $ge_0\notin\{\pm e_0\}$.

\textup{(iv)} If $G_S\supseteq B_4$, then $G_S$ is finite or equal to
$\mathrm O(4)$, and it equals $\mathrm O(4)$ if and only if it contains an
element of infinite order. One element of finite order outside $B_4$ does
not suffice.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) $G_S$ is a group. If $R_m\to R$ with $R_m\in G_S$, the functions
$f\circ R_m^{-1}$ converge uniformly to $f\circ R^{-1}$ with supports in one
cube, and the bound of Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(i) gives $R\in G_S$. So
$G_S$ is a compact Lie group~\cite{Bum13}, and its Lie algebra
$\mathfrak g\subset\mathfrak{so}(4)$ is invariant under $\mathrm{Ad}(g)$,
$g\in\Gamma$. Let $E_{ij}=e_ie_j^{\mathsf T}-e_je_i^{\mathsf T}$. The
diagonal sign changes $D_\epsilon$, $\epsilon\in\{\pm1\}^4$, lie in
$\Gamma$, and $\mathrm{Ad}(D_\epsilon)E_{ij}=\epsilon_i\epsilon_jE_{ij}$. The
six characters $\epsilon\mapsto\epsilon_i\epsilon_j$ are distinct, so the
operators $2^{-4}\sum_\epsilon\epsilon_i\epsilon_j\mathrm{Ad}(D_\epsilon)$
project $\mathfrak g$ onto $\mathfrak g\cap\R E_{ij}$, and $\mathfrak g$ is
spanned by the $E_{ij}$ it contains. The permutations of the spatial axes
act transitively on the pairs $\{i,j\}\subset\{1,2,3\}$ and on the pairs
$\{0,i\}$. So $\mathfrak g$ is $0$, $\mathfrak{so}(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}=
\operatorname{span}\{E_{ij}:i,j\ge1\}$, $\operatorname{span}\{E_{0i}\}$ or
$\mathfrak{so}(4)$, and the third is not a Lie algebra, since
$[E_{01},E_{02}]=-E_{12}$. A connected Lie subgroup is determined by its
Lie algebra.

(ii) If $R\in\SO(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}$ has infinite order, $G_S$ is infinite,
hence of positive dimension, and $G_S\cap(\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1))$
has the same property, so its identity component is a connected subgroup
of $\SO(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}$ of positive dimension normalised by the signed
permutations, and by the argument of (i), applied within
$\mathfrak{so}(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}$, it is $\SO(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}$. With
$\Gamma$ this gives $\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$. The converse is clear.

(iii) If $G_S$ is infinite, its identity component is
$\SO(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}$ or $\SO(4)$ by (i). In the first case it is normal
in $G_S$, and every $g\in G_S$ maps the vectors fixed by
$\SO(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}$, the line $\R e_0$, to the vectors fixed by
$g\SO(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}g^{-1}=\SO(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}$, so $ge_0=\pm e_0$. Hence
an infinite $G_S$ with some $ge_0\ne\pm e_0$ contains $\SO(4)$, and with
$\theta$ it is $\mathrm O(4)$. A rotation $R$ of infinite order with
$Re_0\ne\pm e_0$ makes $G_S$ infinite and supplies $g$; the converses are
clear.

(iv) The sign changes and all permutations of the four axes act on the
pairs transitively, so $\mathfrak g$ is $0$ or $\mathfrak{so}(4)$. The
reflection $s$ in the hyperplane orthogonal to $u=\frac12(1,1,1,1)$, with
