$B_4$, generates a group that permutes the $24$ vectors $\pm e_\mu$ and
$\frac12(\pm1,\pm1,\pm1,\pm1)$: $s$ maps $e_\mu$ to $e_\mu-u$, and $v$ to
$v-2\ip vuu$ with $\ip vu\in\{0,\pm\frac12,\pm1\}$ for the other vectors.
These vectors span $\R^4$, so the group is finite.
\end{proof}

The spatial flow commutes with spatial rotations in the continuum, and in
the free field it leaves exactly $\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$
(Proposition~\ref{prop:osobstruction}(c)). So for the spatially flowed
fields at a positive flow time the symmetry to ask for is
$\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$, and by part (ii) one rotation suffices:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(W)},leftmargin=*]
\item \emph{One spatial rotation.} There is a rotation $R$ of the spatial
coordinates, of infinite order, with
\[
\lim_{k\to\infty}\Bigl[\omega_k\Bigl(\prod_jZ_kA_k(f_j\circ R^{-1})\Bigr)
-\omega_k\Bigl(\prod_jZ_kA_k(f_j)\Bigr)\Bigr]=0
\]
for all $n$ and $f_j\in C_c(\R^4)$.
\end{enumerate}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}, (W) holds if and only
if every subsequential limit is invariant under $\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm
O(1)$: (W) puts $R$ in each $G_S$; conversely, if the difference in (W) stayed
above $\varepsilon$ along a subsequence for some $f_j$, a further
subsequence would give a limit $S$ with $R\notin G_S$. Full Euclidean
invariance, as (E) asks, is not available for these fields at a positive
flow time; it has to be posed for observables whose regularisation is
$\mathrm O(4)$-covariant, such as the four-dimensional flow, which at
positive flow time mixes the two sides of the reflection plane
(Sec.~\ref{ssec:flowlimit}), or the limit of vanishing flow time. For
families invariant under $B_4$ part (iv) applies, and one rotation of
infinite order gives $\mathrm O(4)$. In either case the rotation enters as a
lattice statement: a Ward identity in which the breaking of the rotation is
an insertion that vanishes in the limit, as the symmetry restoration of
Symanzik's improvement programme predicts. It is not proved here.

\emph{Convergence of the whole sequence.}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(U)},leftmargin=*]
\item \emph{One limit.} Any two subsequential limits of
Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(i) coincide.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:uniquelimit}
Assume the hypotheses of Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:osaxioms}}.

\textup{(i)} The moments $\omega_k(\prod_jZ_kA_k(f_j))$ converge for all
$n$ and $f_j\in C_c(\R^4)$ if and only if \textup{(U)} holds.

\textup{(ii)} Let $(\beta_k)$ and $(\beta'_k)$ satisfy the hypothesis of
Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:transfer}} with the same $\alpha,\ell$, and
\textup{(M)} and \textup{(W)} with the same $M_n(Q)$ and $R$, and let
$\beta''_k$ be $\beta_k$ for even $k$ and $\beta'_k$ for odd $k$. Then
$(\beta''_k)$ satisfies the same hypotheses, and if the moments along
$(\beta_k)$ and along $(\beta'_k)$ converge to different limits, those along
$(\beta''_k)$ do not converge.

\textup{(iii)} Suppose that every subsequential limit lies in a set
$\mathfrak F$ of families on which $S\mapsto S_2(\bar f_0\circ\theta,
f_0(\cdot-(\sigma_0,0)))$ is injective, for a dyadic $\sigma_0>0$, and that
$\rho_k(0,\sigma_0)=C_k(\sigma_0)/C_k(0)$ converges. Then \textup{(U)}
holds.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) If a moment does not converge, it has two limit points along two
subsequences, and refining each by Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(i) gives two
different limits. The converse is clear. (ii) The hypotheses are
conditions at each $k$ with constants uniform in $k$, and the even and the
odd terms have different limits. (iii) Along a subsequence with limit $S$,
$S_2(\bar f_0\circ\theta,f_0(\cdot-(\sigma_0,0)))=\lim Z_k^2C_k(\sigma_0)=
\lim\rho_k(0,\sigma_0)$ by the approximation of $\theta$ in the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:higher}(iii), the same number for every subsequence.
\end{proof}

Part (ii) shows that (U) is a property of the tuning that the other
hypotheses do not contain. Part (iii) states it in the form that Yang--Mills theory requires:
if every limit is determined up to the choice of one physical scale, a
single renormalisation condition fixes that scale. For Yang--Mills theory
this is the statement that the continuum theory has no free dimensionless
parameter; it is not proved here.

\emph{What these inputs cannot give.} Gaussian fields show the limits of
the inputs. They are not lattice gauge theories, and the hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer} concerns the Wilson measure in the forest gauge;
but the proofs of Theorems~\ref{thm:osaxioms} and~\ref{thm:ostree} use it
only through the conclusions of Theorem~\ref{thm:onescale}, namely
translation and lattice-symmetry invariance, reflection positivity and the
transfer-matrix gap $m_*a_k$, and the fields below have these.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:osobstruction}
Let $m>0$, $q_k=e^{-ma_k}$, and $f_0\ge0$.

\textup{(a)} Let $\phi_k$ be the centred Gaussian field on $\Z^4$ with
covariance $\prod_{\mu=0}^3q_k^{|x_\mu-y_\mu|}$, and let $A_k(f)=\sum_x
a_k^4f(a_kx)\phi_k(x)$, with $C_k$, $Z_k$ formed from $f_0$ as before. Its
law is invariant under translations and $B_4$ and reflection positive for
the reflections in coordinate hyperplanes through sites and bisecting
links; its transfer matrix in each coordinate direction has spectrum
$\{q_k^N:N\ge0\}$ with $1$ a simple eigenvalue, so its gap is $ma_k$. For
large $k$, \textup{(T1)--(T3)} hold with $r_k=1$, $w_k(z)=\prod_\mu
q_k^{|z_\mu|}$, $K_2=1$ and $K_n=0$ for $n\ge3$. The moments converge along
the whole sequence to the Gaussian family with $S_2(f,g)=\kappa\iint
f(x)g(y)e^{-m\|x-y\|_1}dx\,dy$, $\kappa>0$. Its group $G_S$ is exactly
$B_4$, its Hamiltonian has $\sigma(H)\subset\{0\}\cup[m,\infty)$ with
$m\in\sigma(H)$, and the joint spectrum of $H$ and the spatial momentum
contains $\{m\}\times\R^3$.

\textup{(b)} Let $\psi_k$ be the centred Gaussian field on $\Z^4$ with
covariance $q_k^{|x_0-y_0|}$, and form $A_k$, $C_k$, $Z_k$ in the same way.
Its law is invariant under translations and $\Gamma$, reflection positive
in time, and its transfer matrix has gap $ma_k$. \textup{(M)}, the bound of
Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:osaxioms}(vii)} and $(\mathrm N_0)$ hold, and the
moments converge along the whole sequence; the limit has $U(x)=1$ for every
$x\in\R^3$, so it does not cluster in space. \textup{(T3)} fails.

\textup{(c)} For $t_f>0$ and $\mu>0$, the centred Gaussian field on $\R^4$
with covariance $\widehat C(p)=e^{-2t_f|\mathbf p|^2}(|p|^2+\mu^2)^{-1}$, the
massive free field flowed by the spatial heat flow for the time $t_f$, is
reflection positive and invariant under translations, and its group $G_S$
is exactly $\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) The covariance is a product of the one-dimensional covariances
$q^{|n|}$, which are positive definite, their Fourier transforms being
$(1-q^2)/(1-2q\cos p+q^2)>0$; invariance under translations and $B_4$ is
clear. Fix a coordinate direction as time. The field at time $0$ has a
Gaussian law $\gamma$ with covariance $\prod_{i\ge1}q^{|x_i-y_i|}$, and the
stationary chain $\phi_{t+1}=q\phi_t+(1-q^2)^{1/2}\xi_{t+1}$ with $\xi_t$
independent of law $\gamma$ has covariance $q^{|t-s|}$ times that of
$\gamma$, so it is the field. It is a reversible Markov chain with
transition operator $Pg(\phi)=\E\,g(q\phi+(1-q^2)^{1/2}\xi)$ on
$L^2(\gamma)$. For $h$ of finite support and $\mathcal E_h(\phi)=
\exp(\phi(h)-\frac12\E\phi(h)^2)$ one computes $P\mathcal E_h=\mathcal
E_{qh}$; the part of $\mathcal E_h$ homogeneous of degree $N$ in $h$ spans
the $N$-th Wiener chaos, so $P$ is multiplication by $q^N$ on it, and these
chaoses span $L^2(\gamma)$~\cite[Sect.~6.3]{GJ87}. So $0\le P\le1$ with
spectrum $\{q^N\}$ and $1$ simple. For $F,F'$ functions of the field at
times $\ge0$, the Markov property and reversibility give
$\E[\vartheta(\bar F)F']=\ip{g_F}{g_{F'}}_{L^2(\gamma)}$ with
$g_F=\E[F\mid\phi_0=\cdot]$, and for the reflection bisecting the links
between times $0$ and $1$, with $F,F'$ at times $\ge1$,
$\ip{g_F}{Pg_{F'}}$ with $g_F=\E[F\mid\phi_1=\cdot]$; both are positive
for $F=F'$, and the transfer matrix is $P$. Gaussian fields have vanishing
cumulants of order $n\ge3$, and the covariance is $w_k(x-y)$, which is
(T1). Further $a_k^4W_k=a_k^4((1+q_k)/(1-q_k))^4\to(2/m)^4$, which is (T3),
and since $q_k^{|n|}=e^{-ma_k|n|}$, $C_k(0)$ is a Riemann sum converging to
$\iint f_0(x)f_0(y)e^{-m\|\theta x-y\|_1}dx\,dy>0$, which gives (T2) for
large $k$ and $Z_k\to\kappa^{1/2}$ with $\kappa$ the inverse of this
integral. The moments are sums of products of covariances, Riemann sums
converging to the stated ones. Invariance of a Gaussian family under $R$ is
invariance of $S_2$, that is $e^{-m\|Rz\|_1}=e^{-m\|z\|_1}$ for all $z$; a
linear isometry of the norm $\|\cdot\|_1$ maps the extreme points $\pm
e_\mu$ of its unit ball to themselves, so $R\in B_4$. The spectral bound
follows from the lattice gap $ma_k$ as in Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(vi).
For $f=h\otimes g$ with $h$ supported in $\{t>0\}$ and $\int he^{-mt}dt\ne0$,
$\ip{\Psi(f)}{e^{-sH}U(x)\Psi(f)}$ equals $e^{-ms}$ times a function of $x$
whose Fourier transform is a positive multiple of $|\hat g(\mathbf p)|^2
\prod_{i=1}^32m/(p_i^2+m^2)$. So the joint spectral measure at $\Psi(f)$ is
carried by $\{m\}\times\R^3$, with support $\{m\}\times\R^3$ when $\hat g$
has no zeros.

(b) The field is $\psi_k(x)=X(x_0)$ for the chain of (a) with
$\gamma=N(0,1)$, which gives the symmetry, reflection positivity and the
gap. Write $F_f(t)=\int f(t,\mathbf x)\,d\mathbf x$. The covariance of two
packets converges to $\iint F_f(t)F_g(s)e^{-m|t-s|}dt\,ds$, which is
positive for $f=\bar f_0\circ\theta$, $g=f_0$, and bounded by
$|Q|^2\|f\|_\infty\|g\|_\infty$ for $f,g$ supported in $Q$; with the
Gaussian moment formula this gives (M) with $M_n(Q)$ a constant times
$|Q|^n$, and convergence. The limiting covariance does not change when the
test functions of one factor are translated in space, so neither does any
moment, and $\ip{\Psi(P)}{U(x)\Psi(P')}=\ip{\Psi(P)}{\Psi(P')}$. The ratio
$C_k(\sigma)/C_k(0)$ converges to a positive number, which is
$(\mathrm N_0)$, and $\sum_zq_k^{|z_0|}=\infty$.

(c) For $f$ supported in $\{t>0\}$, the $p_0$ integral gives
\begin{multline*}
\iint\bar f(\theta x)C(x-y)f(y)\,dx\,dy\\
=\int e^{-2t_f|\mathbf p|^2}
(2\omega)^{-1}\Bigl|\int\hat f(t,\mathbf p)e^{-\omega t}dt\Bigr|^2
d\mathbf p\ge0,
\end{multline*}
with $\omega=(|\mathbf p|^2+\mu^2)^{1/2}$ and $\hat f$ the spatial Fourier
transform; reflection positivity of the Gaussian measure
follows~\cite[Sect.~6.2]{GJ87}. An orthogonal $R$ preserves $\widehat C$ if
and only if $|(Rp)^{\mathrm{sp}}|=|p^{\mathrm{sp}}|$ for every $p$, the
spatial parts, because $|Rp|=|p|$ and $s\mapsto e^{-2t_fs}$ is injective;
that is, $R$ preserves $|p_0|$, so $R^{\mathsf T}e_0=\pm e_0$.
\end{proof}

Example (a) is reflection positive, has the gap in every direction and the
lattice symmetry, satisfies the tree bounds with the best constants, and
converges; its limit is invariant under no rotation outside $B_4$, so (W)
does not follow from these properties. Its one-particle energy $m$ does not
depend on the momentum, so the spectral condition of the Wightman axioms,
energy at least $|\mathbf p|$, fails; the gap transfers to
$H\ge m_*$ on $\Omega^\perp$ and to the joint spectrum of
Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(vi), and the spectral condition needs Lorentz
covariance, which only $\mathrm O(4)$ invariance supplies. Example (b)
shows that the time gap and (M) do not give spatial clustering. By
Proposition~\ref{prop:uniquelimit}(ii), example (a) at two masses,
interleaved, satisfies the hypotheses of Theorems~\ref{thm:osaxioms}
and~\ref{thm:ostree} with uniform constants and has two limits.

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:osexact}
Assume the hypothesis of Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:transfer}}, with $\Phi$
real and $W_3$-invariant. The moments $\omega_k(\prod_jZ_kA_k(f_j))$
converge for every $n$ and all $f_j\in C_c(\R^4)$ to a family invariant
under translations and $\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$ if and only if
\textup{(M)}, \textup{(W)} and \textup{(U)} hold. The limit then satisfies
\textup{(i)--(vi)} of Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:osaxioms}} with $\Gamma$
replaced by $\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$: symmetric, reflection
positive distributions, a Hamiltonian with $\sigma(H)\subset\{0\}\cup
[m_*,\infty)$, $\ker H=\C\Omega$ and $\Omega^\perp\ne0$, and unitary
representations of the spatial translations and rotations commuting with
$H$. If \textup{(T1)--(T3)} hold as well, the limit is tempered and
clusters in every direction, and it satisfies the linear growth condition
of~\cite{OS75} when $K_n\le K^n(n!)^\beta$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Convergence gives bounded moments, hence (M) by
Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}; it gives (U), and with the invariance it gives
(W) for every spatial rotation. Conversely, (M) and (U) give convergence by
Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(i) and Proposition~\ref{prop:uniquelimit}(i),
and (W) gives the invariance by Proposition~\ref{prop:rotations}(ii). The
representation of the spatial rotations is constructed as $V$ in
Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(iv), and its continuity follows as there. The
rest is Theorems~\ref{thm:osaxioms} and~\ref{thm:ostree}.
\end{proof}

So, given (D) along the tuning, the part of (E) that concerns the spatially
flowed fields consists of three statements: (M), an ultraviolet bound on
the products of renormalised packets that contains $(\mathrm N_0)$ and
therefore fails along every tuning that stays in the strong-coupling range
(Theorem~\ref{thm:strongratio}); (W), the restoration of the spatial
rotations; and (U), the uniqueness of the limit along the tuning. Each is
necessary. Example (a) shows that (W) does not follow from (M), (U), the
tree bounds and the conclusions of Theorem~\ref{thm:onescale}, and the
same example at two interleaved masses shows that (U) does not follow from
(M), the tree bounds and those conclusions. None of the three is proved. Euclidean invariance and the Wightman spectral
condition are beyond these fields at a positive flow time.

\emph{Three further routes.} The trial condition of
Corollary~\ref{cor:trial} can be approached in three other ways: through
upper bounds on correlations, through trial states smeared in time, and
through a comparison with the Gaussian theory at weak coupling. The first
never implies $(\mathrm N_u)$. The second removes the lattice
scale from the first condition and leaves a lower bound on a ratio of
correlations at physical times. The third gives $(\mathrm N_u)$ from a
relative comparison at two physical separations; when that comparison is
built scale by scale along the one-loop tuning, it must be exact to first
order in the running coupling at every scale.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:upperbounds}
Let $G=\SU(2)$, let $\beta_{W,k}\in[0,0.348]$ be any couplings, let
$\omega_k$ be the states of Theorems~\ref{thm:su2} and~\ref{thm:blocks}, and
let $m_0$ be as in Theorem~\ref{thm:strongratio}.

\textup{(i)} Let $F,H$ be bounded functions at positive times that are
uniform limits of gauge-invariant local functions, with
$\omega_k(F)=\omega_k(H)=0$. Then for every integer $n\ge0$
\[
\bigl|\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(\bar F)\tau_nH\bigr)\bigr|\le
e^{-m_0n}\|\Psi_F\|\,\|\Psi_H\|\le e^{-m_0n}\|F\|_\infty\|H\|_\infty .
\]

\textup{(ii)} Let \textup{(H)} be a set of inequalities indexed by $i\in I$,
each of the form
\[
\bigl|\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(\bar F_{i,k})\tau_{n_{i,k}}H_{i,k}\bigr)\bigr|
\le B_{i,k}\|\Psi_{F_{i,k}}\|\,\|\Psi_{H_{i,k}}\|\qquad(k\ge k_i),
\]
with $F_{i,k},H_{i,k}$ as in \textup{(i)}, integers $n_{i,k}\ge t_i/a_k$ for
some $t_i>0$, and $B_{i,k}>0$ with $a_k\log B_{i,k}\to0$ as $k\to\infty$.
Then each inequality of \textup{(H)} holds for the states $\omega_k$ for all
large $k$, while $(\mathrm N_u)$ fails for every packet with $C_k(0)>0$ and every $u$,
and
every limit of Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:flowlimit}} along these states is
trivial. The same holds with $\|F_{i,k}\|_\infty\|H_{i,k}\|_\infty$ in place
of $\|\Psi_{F_{i,k}}\|\|\Psi_{H_{i,k}}\|$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) Since $\ip\Omega{\Psi_F}=\omega_k(F)=0$ and likewise for $H$, both
vectors lie in $\Omega_k^\perp$, on which $\mathbb T_k$ has norm at most
$e^{-m_0}$, as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:strongratio}. Hence
$|\ip{\Psi_F}{\mathbb T_k^n\Psi_H}|\le e^{-m_0n}\|\Psi_F\|\|\Psi_H\|$, and
$\|\Psi_F\|\le\|F\|_\infty$ by the footnote of Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}.

(ii) Since $a_k\log B_{i,k}\to0$, for large $k$ we have
$a_k\log B_{i,k}\ge-m_0t_i$, that is $B_{i,k}\ge e^{-m_0t_i/a_k}\ge
e^{-m_0n_{i,k}}$, and (i) gives the inequality. The statements on
$(\mathrm N_u)$ and on the limits are Theorem~\ref{thm:strongratio}.
\end{proof}

The class (H) contains uniform bounds $B_{i,k}=B_i$, decay at a physical
rate $B_{i,k}=B_ie^{-Mt_i}$ with any $M$, and bounds of order $a_k^p$, for
connected correlations of any centred observables at physical separations,
flowed or not. The same holds for every compact simple $G$ at couplings in
$[0,\beta_1]$ with $N\beta_1<\frac2{29}\kappa_G$, with $m_0=m_G(\beta_1)$ of
Theorem~\ref{thm:groups}. So a proof of $(\mathrm N_u)$ must contain a lower
bound. In Corollary~\ref{cor:trial} it is $\ip{\chi_k}{\mathbb T_k\chi_k}\ge
1-\kappa a_k$, a lower bound on a normalised correlation at one lattice step
that is accurate to order $a_k$, together with a lower bound on one overlap.
The spectral arguments of Lemma~\ref{lem:trial} and~\cite[Lemma~463]{BBLR26}
turn such a lower bound into a bound on the ratio, but they cannot produce one
from upper bounds: the states above satisfy every inequality of (H), and
the spectral measures of their centred vectors are carried by energies at
least $m_0/a_k$.

\emph{Trial states smeared in time.} A slice function enters
Lemma~\ref{lem:slabdirichlet} through its change in one lattice step, which
Proposition~\ref{prop:lipobstruction} shows to be too large. Smearing the
same function in time over a physical interval replaces that change by a
telescoping sum, in which a lattice step costs $a_k$ times the total
variation of the time profile. Let $\omega$ be a state as in
Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}, with transfer matrix $\mathbb T$ and vacuum
$\Omega$. Let $X$ be a real bounded function of the spatial links of the slice
$x_0=0$, invariant under the gauge transformations of that slice and a
uniform limit of local such functions; by
Proposition~\ref{prop:spatialflow}(ii) this includes $X=N^{-1}\ReTr
W_\gamma\circ\Phi_s$ for the holonomy $W_\gamma$ of a closed path $\gamma$
in the slice and every flow time $s$. Put $X^c=X-\omega(X)$ and
\[
D_X(n)=\omega\bigl(X^c\,\tau_nX^c\bigr)\qquad(n\in\Z_{\ge0}).
\]
Let $w=(w_n)_{n\in\Z}$ be nonnegative, with $w_n=0$ unless $n_0\le n\le n_1$,
where $1\le n_0\le n_1$, and with $\|w\|_1=\sum_nw_n>0$; put
$V(w)=\sum_{n\in\Z}|w_n-w_{n-1}|$ and
\[
B=\sum_nw_n\,\tau_nX^c .
\]

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:smearedtrial}
\textup{(i)} $D_X$ is nonnegative, nonincreasing and log-convex on
$\Z_{\ge0}$, and $\Psi_B\perp\Omega$. If $D_X(2n_1)>0$, then
$\|\Psi_B\|^2\ge\|w\|_1^2D_X(2n_1)>0$, and the unit vector
$\chi=\Psi_B/\|\Psi_B\|$ satisfies
\[
\ip\chi{(1-\mathbb T)\chi}\le\frac{V(w)}{\|w\|_1}\,
\frac{D_X(2n_0)}{D_X(2n_1)} .
\]

\textup{(ii)} If $D_X(2n_1)>0$ and $\ip\chi{(1-\mathbb T)\chi}\le\eta\le
\frac12$, then
$D_X(2n_0+m)\ge e^{-2\eta m}D_X(2n_1)$ for every integer $m\ge0$.

\textup{(iii)} For a function $H$ at positive times as in
Appendix~\textup{\ref{app:transfer}}, $\ip{\Psi_B}{\Psi_H}=
\omega(\vartheta(B)H)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $x=\Psi_{X^c}$. As in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:slabdirichlet}(i),
$\vartheta(X^c)=X^c$ and $\Psi_{\tau_nX^c}=\mathbb T^nx$, so
$D_X(n)=\ip x{\mathbb T^nx}=\int\lambda^nd\nu$ for the spectral measure $\nu$
of $\mathbb T$ at $x$, a finite positive measure on $[0,1]$. Hence $D_X\ge0$,
$D_X$ is nonincreasing, and it is log-convex by H\"older's inequality. Since
$\ip\Omega x=\omega(X^c)=0$ and $\mathbb T\Omega=\Omega$, $x\perp\Omega$ and
so $\Psi_B=W(\mathbb T)x\perp\Omega$, with $W(\lambda)=\sum_nw_n\lambda^n$.

(i) On $[0,1]$, $\|w\|_1\lambda^{n_1}\le W(\lambda)\le\|w\|_1\lambda^{n_0}$,
so $\|\Psi_B\|^2=\int W^2d\nu\ge\|w\|_1^2D_X(2n_1)$. Moreover
\[
W(\lambda)(1-\lambda)=\sum_{n=n_0}^{n_1+1}(w_n-w_{n-1})\lambda^n,
\]
which is nonnegative and at most $V(w)\lambda^{n_0}$ on $[0,1]$. Therefore
\[
\begin{split}
\ip{\Psi_B}{(1-\mathbb T)\Psi_B}&=\int W(\lambda)\cdot W(\lambda)
(1-\lambda)\,d\nu\\
&\le\|w\|_1V(w)D_X(2n_0),
\end{split}
\]
and dividing by $\|\Psi_B\|^2$ gives the bound.

(ii) Let $\nu_\chi=W^2\nu/\int W^2d\nu$ be the spectral measure at $\chi$.
By Jensen's inequality for $\lambda^m$, $\int\lambda^md\nu_\chi\ge
(1-\eta)^m\ge e^{-2\eta m}$, since $\log(1-y)\ge-2y$ on $[0,\frac12]$. By the
bounds on $W$,
\[
\int\lambda^md\nu_\chi=\frac{\int W^2\lambda^md\nu}{\int W^2d\nu}\le
\frac{D_X(2n_0+m)}{D_X(2n_1)} .
\]

(iii) This is the identity of Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}, with
$\vartheta(\bar B)=\vartheta(B)$ because $B$ is real.
\end{proof}

Along a tuning, take $X_k$ as above for $\omega_k$, a time profile $g\ge0$ of
bounded variation supported in $[t_0,t_1]$ with $0<t_0<t_1$ dyadic and
$\int g>0$, and $w_n=a_kg(a_kn)$, so that $B_k=\sum_na_kg(a_kn)\,\tau_nX_k^c$ is the packet of $X_k$ with
time profile $g$; let $\chi_k$ be its unit vector, $\Var(g)$ the total
variation of $g$ on $\R$, and write $D_{X_k}(r)$ for $D_{X_k}(r/a_k)$ when
$r\in a_k\Z_{\ge0}$ is a physical time.

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:smearedtrial}
\textup{(i)} If $D_{X_k}(2t_1)\ge c\,D_{X_k}(2t_0)$ with $c>0$ for all large
$k$, then $\ip{\chi_k}{(1-\mathbb T_k)\chi_k}\le\kappa a_k$ for all large
$k$, with $\kappa=2\Var(g)/(c\int g)$. The hypothesis holds with
$c=c_1^{\max(1,(2t_1-2t_0)/(r'-r))}$ if $D_{X_k}(r')\ge c_1D_{X_k}(r)$ for
dyadic $0<r<r'$ with $r\le2t_0$, a $c_1\in(0,1]$ and all large $k$.

\textup{(ii)} Conversely, if $\ip{\chi_k}{(1-\mathbb T_k)\chi_k}\le\kappa
a_k$ for all large $k$, then $D_{X_k}(2t_0+s)\ge e^{-2\kappa s}D_{X_k}(2t_1)$
for all large $k$ and every $s\in a_k\Z_{\ge0}$; for $s>2(t_1-t_0)$ this is a
lower bound on the ratio of two correlations of $X_k$ at the physical times
$2t_1<2t_0+s$.

\textup{(iii)} If the hypothesis of \textup{(i)} holds and
\[
\bigl|\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(B_k)\,\tau_{u/a_k}A_k\bigr)\bigr|\ge
v\,\|\Psi_{B_k}\|\,C_k(2u)^{1/2}
\]
with $v>0$ for all large $k$, then $(\mathrm N_u)$ holds and
$\rho_k(u,\sigma)\ge(v-\sqrt{\kappa\sigma/2})_+^2$ for $\sigma\ge2a_k$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For large $k$, $t_0/a_k$ and $t_1/a_k$ are integers, and
Proposition~\ref{prop:smearedtrial} applies with $n_0=t_0/a_k$ and
$n_1=t_1/a_k$; $n_0\ge1$. The points $a_kn$ partition $\R$, so
$V(w)=a_k\sum_n|g(a_kn)-g(a_k(n-1))|\le a_k\Var(g)$, while
$\|w\|_1=a_k\sum_ng(a_kn)$ is a Riemann sum of $g$, which is Riemann
integrable, and so is at least $\frac12\int g$ for large $k$. Then (i)
follows from Proposition~\ref{prop:smearedtrial}(i); the condition
$D_{X_k}(2t_1)>0$ holds because $D_{X_k}(2t_0)>0$ is implicit in the
hypothesis. Its second sentence is the log-convexity argument of
Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}(ii) with $\alpha=2t_0$, $\beta=2t_1$,
which uses only that $D_{X_k}$ is nonincreasing and log-convex. For (ii),
$\kappa a_k\le\frac12$ for large $k$, and Proposition~\ref{prop:smearedtrial}(ii)
with $m=s/a_k$ gives the claim. For (iii), Proposition~\ref{prop:smearedtrial}(iii)
gives $|\ip{\chi_k}{\Psi_{k,u}}|\ge v$, and Corollary~\ref{cor:trial} gives
the rest.
\end{proof}

For trial states smeared in time, the first condition of
Corollary~\ref{cor:trial} therefore holds along a tuning if and only if,
up to the choice of the physical times, a ratio of two correlations of the
smeared field at physical times is bounded below. No lattice-scale quantity
enters: no Lipschitz constant, no plaquette energy, and no first step.
Proposition~\ref{prop:lipobstruction} concerns single slices and does not
apply to $B_k$. When $\Phi$ is real, $X_k$ is the slice field $U_k$ of a product packet
$f_0=g\otimes h$ and $u=0$, $B_k=A_k$, the overlap is $1$, and part (i)
gives the hypothesis of Proposition~\ref{prop:flowshift}(ii) from the slice
ratio of Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}(iii), without the comparison of
$u_k$ with the packet that was left open after
Proposition~\ref{prop:flowshift}. For a flowed loop $X_k=N^{-1}\ReTr
W_{\gamma_k}\circ\Phi_{\tau/a_k^2}$, with $\gamma_k$ the lattice
approximation of a closed curve of physical size in the slice, the input
that the route needs is the pair of lower bounds of
Corollary~\ref{cor:smearedtrial}(i) and~(iii): a ratio of two flowed-loop
correlations at physical times, and the overlap of the smeared loop with
the flowed packet at physical distance. Both are lower bounds at physical
separations, of the same kind as $(\mathrm N_u)$, and by
Proposition~\ref{prop:upperbounds} no upper bound supplies them. The overlap
vanishes for every $k$ if $\Psi_{B_k}$ and $\Psi_{A_k}$ lie in different
eigenspaces of a unitary that commutes with $\mathbb T_k$; for instance, if
$G=\SU(N)$ with $N\ge3$ and $\omega_k$ is invariant under charge
conjugation $U\mapsto\bar U$, then $X_k=N^{-1}\operatorname{Im}\Tr
W_{\gamma_k}\circ\Phi_{\tau/a_k^2}$ is odd and the flowed energy packet is
even, so $X_k$ has to be chosen in the symmetry sector of the packet.

\emph{Comparison with the Gaussian theory.} Let $C^{\mathrm G}_k(t)>0$ be the
correlations of the corresponding packet in a comparison theory, such as the
Gaussian theory at tree level, with ratio
$\rho^{\mathrm G}_k=C^{\mathrm G}_k(2u+\sigma)/C^{\mathrm G}_k(2u)$. If there
are $Z_k>0$ and $\delta_k\in[0,1)$ such that for $t=2u$ and $t=2u+\sigma$
\[
(1-\delta_k)Z_kC^{\mathrm G}_k(t)\le C_k(t)\le(1+\delta_k)Z_kC^{\mathrm
G}_k(t),
\]
then dividing the lower bound at $2u+\sigma$ by the upper bound at $2u$ gives
\[
\rho_k(u,\sigma)\ge\frac{1-\delta_k}{1+\delta_k}\,\rho^{\mathrm G}_k .
\]
The normalisation $Z_k$ cancels, and only the two physical separations $2u$
and $2u+\sigma$ enter. For the free fields of Remarks~\ref{rem:freeslice}
and~\ref{rem:compositeslice}, the slice ratios have positive limits, so
$\liminf_k\rho^{\mathrm G}_k>0$ by the argument of
Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}(iii), which uses only reflection positivity
and the transfer matrix,
and $(\mathrm N_u)$ follows from $\limsup_k\delta_k<1$. This relative
comparison at two physical separations, uniform in $k$, is what a
perturbative lower bound has to provide. It is not a statement of fixed order
in the bare coupling: along the one-loop tuning $b\log(\sigma/a_k)/\beta_k\to
1$, so the terms $(\log(\sigma/a_k)/\beta_k)^n$ of bare perturbation theory
are of order one at every $n$. The comparison has to be organised by
scales, as in \cite[Proposition~460]{BBLR26}, where repeating a
comparison over scales multiplies the factors $1\pm\varepsilon$. The next
proposition determines which losses per scale the one-loop tuning allows.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:scalelosses}
Let $b>0$, $h=b\log2$, $a_k=2^{-k}a_0$, and let the couplings satisfy
$|\beta_k-b\log(1/a_k)|\le M$ for all $k$. Let $\sigma>0$ be dyadic with
$\beta^-_\sigma=b\log(1/\sigma)-M>0$, put $\beta^+_\sigma=b\log(1/\sigma)+M$,
and for $k$ with $\sigma/a_k=2^{J_k}$, $J_k\in\Z_{\ge0}$, put
\[
\beta^{(j)}_k=\beta_k-hj\qquad(0\le j\le J_k),
\]
the one-loop coupling at the scale $2^ja_k$. Let $c_{k,j}\in[0,\frac12]$.

\textup{(i)} $\beta^-_\sigma+h(J_k-j)\le\beta^{(j)}_k\le\beta^+_\sigma+
h(J_k-j)$.

\textup{(ii)} If $c_{k,j}\ge c/\beta^{(j)}_k$ for all $j$, with $c>0$, then
\[
\prod_{j=0}^{J_k}(1-c_{k,j})\le\Bigl(\frac{\beta^+_\sigma}{\beta^+_\sigma+
b\log(2\sigma/a_k)}\Bigr)^{c/h},
\]
which tends to $0$ as $k\to\infty$.

\textup{(iii)} If $c_{k,j}\le c/(\beta^{(j)}_k)^2$ for all $j$, then for
every $k$
\[
\prod_{j=0}^{J_k}(1-c_{k,j})\ge\exp\Bigl(-2c\Bigl(\frac1{(\beta^-_\sigma)^2}
+\frac1{h\beta^-_\sigma}\Bigr)\Bigr).
\]
More generally, $\prod_j(1-c_{k,j})\ge e^{-2\sum_jc_{k,j}}$, and the
products are bounded below uniformly in $k$ if and only if the sums
$\sum_jc_{k,j}$ are bounded.

For $\SU(2)$ and the one-loop tuning, $b=11/(3\pi^2)$ and
$h\in[0.25751,0.25752]$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) Since $\log(1/a_k)=\log(1/\sigma)+J_k\log2$, the hypothesis gives
$\beta^-_\sigma+hJ_k\le\beta_k\le\beta^+_\sigma+hJ_k$; subtract $hj$.

(ii) By $1-y\le e^{-y}$ and (i), with $i=J_k-j$,
\[
\begin{split}
\sum_{j=0}^{J_k}c_{k,j}&\ge c\sum_{i=0}^{J_k}\frac1{\beta^+_\sigma+hi}\ge
c\int_0^{J_k+1}\frac{dt}{\beta^+_\sigma+ht}\\
&=\frac ch\log\frac{\beta^+_\sigma+h(J_k+1)}{\beta^+_\sigma},
\end{split}
\]
since the integrand decreases, and $h(J_k+1)=b\log(2\sigma/a_k)\to\infty$.

(iii) Since $\log(1-y)\ge-2y$ on $[0,\frac12]$,
$\prod_j(1-c_{k,j})\ge e^{-2\sum_jc_{k,j}}$, and $\prod_j(1-c_{k,j})\le
e^{-\sum_jc_{k,j}}$ gives the converse of the last statement. By (i),
\[
\sum_{j=0}^{J_k}\frac1{(\beta^{(j)}_k)^2}\le\sum_{i\ge0}\frac1{(\beta^-_\sigma
+hi)^2}\le\frac1{(\beta^-_\sigma)^2}+\int_0^\infty\frac{dt}{(\beta^-_\sigma+
ht)^2},
\]
and the integral is $1/(h\beta^-_\sigma)$. For $\SU(2)$, $h=b\log2=11\log2/
(3\pi^2)$, and $0.6931471805<\log2<0.6931471806$, from the series of
$2\operatorname{artanh}\frac13$, with $3.1415926535<\pi<3.1415926536$ give
$h\in[0.2575118,0.2575119]$, so $4/h<15.534$.
\end{proof}

With $g_j^2=4/\beta^{(j)}_k$, part (ii) says that a scale-by-scale
comparison that loses a factor $1-c\,g_j^2$ at every scale between $a_k$ and
$\sigma$, however small $c>0$, gives a lower bound on $\rho_k(u,\sigma)$ that
tends to zero, at the rate $(\log(1/a_k))^{-4c/h}$, with $4/h<15.534$;\footnote{Since $\beta_k=b\log(1/a_k)+O(1)$, this rate is a constant times $(g_0^2)^{4c/h}$ with $g_0^2=4/\beta_k$ the bare coupling: a power of the coupling at the lattice scale, which is not small at any fixed order of perturbation theory in $g_0^2$ but tends to zero along the tuning.} such a
bound carries no information in the limit. Part (iii) says that losses of
second order in the running coupling give $\rho_k(u,\sigma)\ge
\rho^{\mathrm G}_ke^{-2c(1/(\beta^-_\sigma)^2+1/(h\beta^-_\sigma))}$ uniformly
in $k$, a bound that approaches $\rho^{\mathrm G}_k$ as $\sigma$ decreases.
So a perturbative lower bound on the ratio along the one-loop tuning has to
account exactly for the first-order effect of every scale on the ratio,
leaving errors whose sum over the scales is bounded. A first-order effect
common to $C_k(2u)$ and $C_k(2u+\sigma)$ cancels in the ratio, and the proof
has to exhibit that cancellation; bounding each correlation separately to
first order is not enough. This is the summability of the one-step errors in
\cite[Proposition~460]{BBLR26}, now for the ratio, and the effective
actions it requires are item~(iv) of~\cite[Sec.~XIII\,V]{BBLR26}, the
renormalisation group analysis of Ba{\l}aban~\cite{Bal84a,Bal85a,Bal89b},
which is not carried out here. None of the three routes gives
$(\mathrm N_u)$ along the one-loop tuning without an input of this kind:
a lower bound on correlations at physical separations, for the packet, for
a smeared field that overlaps it, or for the Gaussian comparison.

\begin{figure*}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{figures/fig_scalelosses.png}
\caption{Products of the factors $1-c_j$ over the $J+1$ scales between
$a_k$ and $\sigma$ along the one-loop $\SU(2)$ tuning, with $\beta_W=20$ at
the scale $\sigma$, as in Proposition~\ref{prop:scalelosses}. (a) Losses of
first order in the running coupling, $c_j=c\,g_j^2$, drive the product to
zero for every $c>0$; losses of second order, $c_j=c\,g_j^4$, keep it
bounded below. (b) The section $c=0.1$, with the bound of part (ii)
dashed.}
\label{fig:scalelosses}
\end{figure*}

\emph{Reductions of (M), (W) and (U).} The rest of this subsection reduces
each of the three statements to a lattice statement and determines what the
bounds of this paper give for it. The constant $Z_k$ is fixed by the
reflected norm $C_k(0)$, while the moments in (M) are not reflected. Let
\[
V_k=\omega_k\bigl(|A_k|^2\bigr),\qquad
\Upsilon_k=Z_k^2V_k=\frac{V_k}{C_k(0)} ,
\]
the variance of the packet of $f_0$ and its ratio to the reflected norm,
and consider:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$)},leftmargin=*]
\item For every $n\ge1$ and every cube $Q$ there are
$M^{\mathrm{loc}}_n(Q)<\infty$ and $k_0$ with
\[
\Bigl|\omega_k\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^nA_k(f_j)\Bigr)\Bigr|\le
M^{\mathrm{loc}}_n(Q)\,V_k^{n/2}\prod_{j=1}^n\|f_j\|_\infty
\]
for $k\ge k_0$ and $f_j\in C_c(\R^4)$ supported in $Q$.
\end{enumerate}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(M$_Z$)},leftmargin=*]
\item $\sup_k\Upsilon_k<\infty$.
\end{enumerate}
Statement (M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$) is (M) with $Z_k$ replaced by the local
normalisation $V_k^{-1/2}$; it does not change when $\Phi$ is multiplied
by a constant, and it does not involve the reflection.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:msplit}
Let the states $\omega_k$ be invariant under translations and under
$\vartheta$ and reflection positive, and let $\Phi$ be real.

\textup{(i)} $\Upsilon_k\ge1$, and for $f_j$ supported in a cube $Q$
\[
\Bigl|\omega_k\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^nZ_kA_k(f_j)\Bigr)\Bigr|\le
\bigl(2\|\Phi\|_\infty Z_k|Q|_k\bigr)^n\prod_{j=1}^n\|f_j\|_\infty .
\]
In particular \textup{(M)} holds if $\sup_kZ_k<\infty$.

\textup{(ii)} \textup{(M)} holds if and only if \textup{(M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$)}
and \textup{(M$_Z$)} hold. Precisely, \textup{(M)} gives
\textup{(M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$)} with $M^{\mathrm{loc}}_n=M_n$ and
$\Upsilon_k\le M_2(Q_0)\|f_0\|_\infty^2$ for a cube $Q_0$ containing the
support of $f_0$, and \textup{(M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$)} and \textup{(M$_Z$)}
give \textup{(M)} with $M_n=M^{\mathrm{loc}}_n\sup_k\Upsilon_k^{n/2}$.

\textup{(iii)} Assume \textup{(T1)}. Then \textup{(T2)} holds if and only
if \textup{(M$_Z$)} holds and $V_k\ge c'r_k^2a_k^4W_k$ for a $c'>0$ and
all $k$. If \textup{(T1)}, \textup{(T3)} and this lower bound on $V_k$
hold, then \textup{(M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$)} holds.

\textup{(iv)} Let $f_0=g\otimes h$ be a product packet as in
Proposition~\textup{\ref{prop:sliceratio}}, choose an interval $I$ of
length $\ell_I>0$ and $c_g>0$ with $g\ge c_g$ on $I$, and put
\[
\begin{split}
&\kappa_g=\tfrac12c_g^2\ell_I(1-e^{-1}),\quad
\gamma_g=(t_1-t_0+\ell_I)\|g\|_\infty,\\
&\Lambda_g=\max\Bigl(\frac4{\ell_I},\frac1{2t_0}\Bigr),\quad
\varepsilon_g(\Lambda)=\frac{\gamma_g^2\Lambda e^{-2\Lambda t_0}}
{\kappa_g}.
\end{split}
\]
Assume that the states have the transfer matrices of
Appendix~\textup{\ref{app:transfer}}. If $a_k\le\ell_I/4$, then for every
$\Lambda\ge\Lambda_g$ and every $\sigma\in a_k\Z_{\ge0}$
\[
\rho_k(0,\sigma)\ge e^{-\Lambda\sigma}\bigl(1-\varepsilon_g(\Lambda)
\Upsilon_k\bigr).
\]
Hence \textup{(M$_Z$)} implies $(\mathrm N_u)$ for every dyadic $u\ge0$.

\textup{(v)} Let $G=\SU(2)$, $\beta_{W,k}\in[0,0.348]$ and $\omega_k$ the
states of Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:strongratio}}, and let $f_0$ be as in
\textup{(iv)}. Then for $a_k\le\min(\ell_I/4,m_0/(2\Lambda_g))$
\[
\Upsilon_k\ge\bigl(1-e^{-m_0/2}\bigr)\frac{2a_k\kappa_g}{m_0\gamma_g^2}
\,e^{m_0t_0/a_k} .
\]

\textup{(vi)} Let $m>0$, $q_k=e^{-ma_k}$, let $\psi_k$ be the field of
Proposition~\textup{\ref{prop:osobstruction}(a)}, $\xi_k$ a family of
independent standard normal variables on $\Z^4$ independent of $\psi_k$,
$\phi_k=\psi_k+a_k^{-5/2}\xi_k$, $A_k(f)=\sum_xa_k^4f(a_kx)\phi_k(x)$, and
$f_0\ge0$ nonzero. The law of $\phi_k$ is invariant under translations and
$B_4$ and reflection positive for the reflections in coordinate
hyperplanes through sites and bisecting links, its transfer matrix in each
coordinate direction has gap $ma_k$, $\rho_k(u,\sigma)=e^{-m\sigma}$ for
all $u,\sigma$, and $Z_k$ converges; but $\Upsilon_k\to\infty$, so
\textup{(M)} fails for $n=2$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) Since $\vartheta$ is an automorphism with $\overline{\vartheta(F)}=
\vartheta(\bar F)$ and $\omega_k$ is $\vartheta$-invariant,
$\omega_k(|\vartheta(\bar A_k)|^2)=\omega_k(\vartheta(|A_k|^2))=V_k$, and
the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality gives $C_k(0)\le V_k$. Further
$|A_k(f)|\le2\|\Phi\|_\infty a_k^4\#\{x:a_kx\in Q\}\|f\|_\infty$.

(ii) Since $\Phi$ is real, $A_k(\bar f_0)=\overline{A_k(f_0)}$, and (M)
with $n=2$, $f_1=\bar f_0$ and $f_2=f_0$ is the bound on $\Upsilon_k$. By
(i), $Z_k^{-n}=C_k(0)^{n/2}\le V_k^{n/2}$, which gives
(M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$). Conversely, since $Z_k^nV_k^{n/2}=\Upsilon_k^{n/2}$,
\[
\Bigl|\omega_k\Bigl(\prod_jZ_kA_k(f_j)\Bigr)\Bigr|\le
M^{\mathrm{loc}}_n\Upsilon_k^{n/2}\prod_j\|f_j\|_\infty .
\]

(iii) The packets are centred, so $V_k$ is the joint cumulant of
$A_k(\bar f_0)$ and $A_k(f_0)$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:treesum} gives
$V_k\le K_2r_k^2|Q_0|_ka_k^4W_k\|f_0\|_\infty^2$. If (T2) holds, then
$V_k\ge C_k(0)\ge c\,r_k^2a_k^4W_k$ and $\Upsilon_k\le K_2|Q_0|_k
\|f_0\|_\infty^2/c$, which is bounded since $|Q_0|_k\le(L+a_0)^4$ for a
cube of side $L$. Conversely $C_k(0)=V_k/\Upsilon_k\ge(c'/\sup_j\Upsilon_j)
r_k^2a_k^4W_k$. For (M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$), Lemma~\ref{lem:treesum} and the
lower bound give for a cumulant of order $p\ge2$
\[
\begin{split}
&\bigl|\omega^T_k\bigl(A_k(f_1);\dots;A_k(f_p)\bigr)\bigr|\\
&\quad\le K_pp^{p-2}c'^{-p/2}B_*^{\,p/2-1}|Q|_k\,V_k^{p/2}
\prod_{j=1}^p\|f_j\|_\infty ,
\end{split}
\]
using (T3), and a moment is the sum over the partitions of
$\{1,\dots,n\}$ without singletons of the products of the cumulants of the
blocks, each partition contributing a factor $V_k^{n/2}$.

(iv) We use the notation of the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}: $A_k=\sum_nw_n\tau_nU_k$ with
$w_n=a_kg(a_kn)$, $\nu$ the spectral measure of $\mathbb T_k$ at $u_k$,
$W(\lambda)=\sum_nw_n\lambda^n$, and $C_k(\sigma)=\int W^2\lambda^{\sigma/a_k}
d\nu$. The function $U_k$ depends only on the spatial links of the slice
at time $0$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:spatialflow}(i)), which $\vartheta$
fixes, so for $j\ge0$, by translation invariance,
\[
\omega_k\bigl(\bar U_k\,\tau_jU_k\bigr)=\omega_k\bigl(\vartheta(\bar U_k)
\tau_jU_k\bigr)=\int\lambda^jd\nu ,
\]
and the same holds for $j<0$ with $|j|$ in place of $j$, the integral
being real. Hence
\[
V_k=\int\mathcal Q\,d\nu,\qquad
\mathcal Q(\lambda)=\sum_{n,n'}w_nw_{n'}\lambda^{|n-n'|} .
\]
Write $\lambda=e^{-a_kE}$ with $E\in[0,\infty]$. First,
$W(\lambda)\le\|w\|_1\lambda^{n_0}\le\gamma_ge^{-Et_0}$, since
$n_0\ge t_0/a_k$ and $\|w\|_1\le(t_1-t_0+a_k)\|g\|_\infty$. Second, let
$J$ be the set of $n$ with $a_kn\in I$; it consists of
$M\ge\ell_I/a_k-1\ge\frac34\ell_I/a_k$ consecutive integers, and $w_n\ge
a_kc_g$ on $J$. Every $n\in J$ has on one side at least $p=\lceil
M/2\rceil-1$ further elements of $J$, so, the terms of $\mathcal Q$ being
nonnegative,
\[
\mathcal Q(\lambda)\ge a_k^2c_g^2M\sum_{j=0}^{p}\lambda^j
\ge a_k^2c_g^2M\,\frac{1-\lambda^{M/2}}{1-\lambda} .
\]
For $E\ge4/\ell_I$, $\lambda^{M/2}\le e^{-3E\ell_I/8}\le e^{-1}$ and
$1-\lambda\le a_kE$, so $\mathcal Q(\lambda)\ge\frac34\ell_Ic_g^2
(1-e^{-1})/E\ge\kappa_g/E$. The function $Ee^{-2Et_0}$ decreases for
$E\ge1/(2t_0)$, so for $\Lambda\ge\Lambda_g$, on the set
$\{E>\Lambda\}$,
\[
W^2\le\frac{\gamma_g^2}{\kappa_g}Ee^{-2Et_0}\mathcal Q\le
\varepsilon_g(\Lambda)\mathcal Q ,
\]
which also holds at $E=\infty$, where $W=0$. Therefore
$\int_{\{E>\Lambda\}}W^2d\nu\le\varepsilon_g(\Lambda)V_k=
\varepsilon_g(\Lambda)\Upsilon_kC_k(0)$, and
\[
C_k(\sigma)\ge e^{-\Lambda\sigma}\int_{\{E\le\Lambda\}}W^2d\nu
\ge e^{-\Lambda\sigma}\bigl(1-\varepsilon_g(\Lambda)\Upsilon_k\bigr)C_k(0).
\]
If $\Upsilon_k\le\Upsilon$ for all $k$, choose $\Lambda\ge\Lambda_g$ with
$\varepsilon_g(\Lambda)\Upsilon\le\frac12$; then $\rho_k(0,\sigma)\ge
\frac12e^{-\Lambda\sigma}$, which is $(\mathrm N_0)$, and
Proposition~\ref{prop:ratioexact}(iii) gives $(\mathrm N_u)$.

(v) By Theorem~\ref{thm:strongratio} with $\sigma=a_k$,
$\rho_k(0,a_k)\le e^{-m_0}$. Part (iv) with $\sigma=a_k$ and
$\Lambda=m_0/(2a_k)\ge\Lambda_g$ gives $e^{-m_0}\ge e^{-m_0/2}(1-
\varepsilon_g(\Lambda)\Upsilon_k)$, so $\Upsilon_k\ge(1-e^{-m_0/2})/
\varepsilon_g(m_0/(2a_k))$, which is the claim.

(vi) The covariance of $\phi_k$ is $\prod_\mu q_k^{|x_\mu-y_\mu|}+
a_k^{-5}\delta_{xy}$, which is invariant under translations and $B_4$.
Fix a coordinate direction as time. The process of the time slices of
$(\psi_k,\xi_k)$ is the stationary reversible Markov chain of the proof
of Proposition~\ref{prop:osobstruction}(a) for $\psi_k$, together with
independent slices of $\xi_k$; its transition operator is $P\otimes\Pi$,
with $P$ as there and $\Pi$ the projection onto the functions that do not
depend on the slice of $\xi_k$. It is positive with spectrum
$\{q_k^N:N\ge0\}\cup\{0\}$ and $1$ simple, and the argument given there
gives reflection positivity and the gap. The packets in $C_k(\sigma)$,
$\sigma\ge0$, are supported at times $x_0\le-1$ and $x_0\ge1$, where
the variables $\xi_k$ are independent and centred, so $C_k(\sigma)$ is
that of example (a): with the time factor $q_k^{y_0-x_0}$, the
substitution $y\mapsto y+(\sigma/a_k)e_0$ gives $C_k(2u+\sigma)=
e^{-m\sigma}C_k(2u)$, and $Z_k$ converges as in (a). The variances add,
so $V_k\ge a_k^{-5}a_k^8\sum_xf_0(a_kx)^2$, which is $a_k^{-1}$ times a
Riemann sum converging to $\|f_0\|_2^2>0$. Hence $\Upsilon_k\to\infty$,
and (M) for $n=2$ fails by (ii).
\end{proof}

By (ii), the renormalisation enters (M) only through (M$_Z$), a comparison
of the variance of the packet with its reflected norm. By the proof of
(iv), for a product packet $V_k$ weighs the spectral measure of the slice
field by $\mathcal Q\ge\kappa_g/E$ at energies $E\ge4/\ell_I$, while
$C_k(0)$ weighs it by $W^2\le\gamma_g^2e^{-2Et_0}$: (M$_Z$) says that the
part of the variance carried at high energy, which the reflection
suppresses over the distance $2t_0$, is bounded by a multiple of the
reflected norm, and it implies $(\mathrm N_0)$. In the strong-coupling
range $\Upsilon_k$ grows exponentially in $1/a_k$ by (v). Example (vi) has
the lattice symmetry, reflection positivity, the gap $ma_k$ and ratios
$\rho_k(u,\sigma)=e^{-m\sigma}$ independent of $k$, and (M$_Z$) fails for
it; so (M$_Z$) does not follow from $(\mathrm N_u)$ and these properties.
By Remark~\ref{rem:markov}, (M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$) does not follow from
bounds on two-point functions. Every inequality that holds, with
constants independent of $k$, for the states of
Theorem~\ref{thm:strongratio} along all sequences of couplings in
$[0,0.348]$ holds along sequences on which (M$_Z$) fails by (v), and (M)
fails with it; so no such inequality implies (M$_Z$) or (M). This applies
to every consequence of reflection positivity at all $\beta_W\ge0$, such
as the chessboard estimates~\cite{FILS78}, and to the bounds of class (H)
of Proposition~\ref{prop:upperbounds}; a proof of (M) has to use that the
couplings leave the strong-coupling range. A sufficient condition without smearing
follows from H\"older's and Minkowski's inequalities and translation
invariance: if
\[
\sup_kZ_k\bigl\|\varphi_k(0)-\omega_k(\varphi_k(0))\bigr\|_{L^n(\omega_k)}
<\infty\qquad(n\ge1),
\]
then $\|Z_kA_k(f)\|_{L^n(\omega_k)}$ is at most $|Q|_k\|f\|_\infty$ times
this supremum, and (M) holds. This pointwise bound on the renormalised
flowed field is not proved.

\emph{The flow does not bound the field.} Part (i) of
Proposition~\ref{prop:msplit} is the bound that uses only
$\|\Phi\|_\infty$. The spatial flow at a positive flow time does not
improve it configuration by configuration.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:stationary}
Let $G=\SU(N)$, $N\ge2$, $\zeta=e^{2\pi i/N}$, and let $U^*$ be the
configuration with all temporal links equal to $1$ and spatial links
\[
U^*_{(x,x+e_1)}=1,\quad U^*_{(x,x+e_2)}=\zeta^{x_1},\quad
U^*_{(x,x+e_3)}=\zeta^{x_1+x_2},
\]
multiples of the identity.

\textup{(i)} Every spatial plaquette of $U^*$ is $\zeta^{\pm1}$, and
$\Phi_s(gU^*)=gU^*$ and $\Phi_s(g1)=g1$ for every gauge transformation
$g$ and every $s\in\R$.

\textup{(ii)} Let $\Phi$ be the sum of $N-\ReTr U_p$ over the twelve
spatial plaquettes with a corner at $0$. Then for all $x$ and $s$
\[
\Phi_x\circ\Phi_s(U^*)=12N\bigl(1-\cos(2\pi/N)\bigr),\qquad
\Phi_x\circ\Phi_s(1)=0 .
\]

\textup{(iii)} Let $\omega(O)=\mu(O^F)$ for gauge-invariant $O$, with
$\mu$ a Gibbs measure of $\gamma^F$ at $\beta\ge0$, as for the states of
Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:osaxioms}}, let $\Phi$ be as in \textup{(ii)} and
$f\ge0$. Then the $\omega$-essential supremum and infimum of $A_k(f)$
differ by at least $12N(1-\cos(2\pi/N))\,a_k^4\sum_xf(a_kx)$, at every
flow time, and
\[
\|Z_kA_k(f)\|_{L^\infty(\omega)}\ge6N\Bigl(1-\cos\frac{2\pi}N\Bigr)Z_k\,
a_k^4\sum_xf(a_kx) .
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) Let $1\le i<j\le3$. The plaquette at $x$ in the plane of $e_i$ and $e_j$ is
\[
U_{(x,x+e_i)}U_{(x+e_i,x+e_i+e_j)}U^{-1}_{(x+e_j,x+e_i+e_j)}U^{-1}_{(x,x+e_j)} .
\]
For $U^*$ and $(i,j)=(1,2),(1,3),(2,3)$ it is
\[
\zeta^{x_1+1}\zeta^{-x_1},\quad\zeta^{x_1+x_2+1}\zeta^{-x_1-x_2},\quad
\zeta^{x_1}\zeta^{x_1+x_2+1}\zeta^{-x_1}\zeta^{-x_1-x_2},
\]
that is $\zeta$; with the opposite orientation it is $\zeta^{-1}$. Let $e$ be a
spatial link and $X$ in the Lie algebra of $\SU(N)$. All links of $U^*$
are central, so replacing $U^*_e$ by $U^*_ee^{\epsilon X}$ changes a
plaquette $p\ni e$ into $c\,e^{\pm\epsilon X}$ with $c=\zeta^{\pm1}$, and
\[
\frac d{d\epsilon}\ReTr\bigl(c\,e^{\pm\epsilon X}\bigr)\Big|_{\epsilon=0}
=\pm\mathrm{Re}\bigl(c\Tr X\bigr)=0 ,
\]
since $\Tr X=0$. So the gradient in the flow equation vanishes at $U^*$,
and $\Phi_s(U^*)=U^*$ by the uniqueness in
Proposition~\ref{prop:spatialflow}(i). The same computation with $c=1$
applies to the configuration $1$, and the flow commutes with gauge
transformations.

(ii) $N-\ReTr(\zeta^{\pm1}1)=N(1-\cos(2\pi/N))$ for each of the twelve
plaquettes, and $N-\ReTr1=0$; by (i) the flow does not move either
configuration.

(iii) As in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:gaugefix}, choosing a base point
on each component of $F$ gives a gauge transformation $g$ with
$(gU^*)_e=1$ on $F$; let $Q^*$ be the restriction of $gU^*$ to the active
links, and $Q^1\equiv1$. The functions $F_x=(\Phi_x\circ\Phi_{s_k})^F$ are
uniform limits of continuous functions of finitely many active links
(Proposition~\ref{prop:spatialflow}(ii)), and gauge invariance gives
$F_x(Q^*)=\Phi_x\circ\Phi_{s_k}(U^*)$. Given $\eta>0$ and the finite set
$X_f$ of $x$ with $f(a_kx)\ne0$, there is a neighbourhood of $Q^*$, fixed
by conditions on finitely many links $B$, on which $|F_x-F_x(Q^*)|<\eta$
for $x\in X_f$, and similarly for $Q^1$. Given the other links, the
conditional law under $\mu$ of the links of $B$ has density
proportional to $\exp(N\beta\sum_p\ReTr Q^F_p)$ with respect to Haar
measure, the sum running over the plaquettes meeting $B$, and this density
is at least $e^{-2N^2\beta|P_B|}$, with $|P_B|$ the number of these
plaquettes. So both neighbourhoods have positive probability. On them
$A_k(f)$ is within $\eta a_k^4\sum_xf(a_kx)$ of its values at $Q^*$ and
$Q^1$, which differ by $12N(1-\cos(2\pi/N))a_k^4\sum_xf(a_kx)$ by (ii).
Since $\eta$ is arbitrary, this gives the first claim, and the
$L^\infty$ norm is at least half the difference.
\end{proof}

So the bound of Proposition~\ref{prop:msplit}(i) is attained up to a
constant factor at every flow time, and it gives (M) only when $Z_k$ is
bounded. Away from the configurations of least action the flow has
stationary points at which the flowed energy density keeps its lattice
size; a proof of (M) by way of the flow has to bound the probability of
the configurations near such points, which is a property of the measure
and not of the flow.

\emph{Rotations as Ward identities.} For $X\in\mathfrak{so}(4)$ and $f\in
C^1_c(\R^4)$ let $L_Xf(x)=\nabla f(x)\cdot Xx$, the derivative of
$f\circ e^{\epsilon X}$ at $\epsilon=0$, and $E_{\mu\nu}$ as in the proof
of Proposition~\ref{prop:rotations}.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:ward}
Let $S$ be a family as in Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:osaxioms}(i)--(ii)},
$X\in\mathfrak{so}(4)$ and $n\ge1$. Then $S_n(f_1\circ e^{-\epsilon X},
\dots,f_n\circ e^{-\epsilon X})=S_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)$ for all $\epsilon\in\R$
and $f_j\in C_c(\R^4)$ if and only if
\[
\sum_{j=1}^nS_n(f_1,\dots,L_Xf_j,\dots,f_n)=0
\]
for all $f_j\in C^\infty_c(\R^4)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $R_\epsilon=e^{\epsilon X}$ and $F(\epsilon)=S_n(f_1\circ R_\epsilon,
\dots,f_n\circ R_\epsilon)$ for $f_j\in C^\infty_c(\R^4)$. Since $R_\epsilon$
commutes with $X$ and $\nabla(f\circ R)(x)=R^{\mathsf T}\nabla f(Rx)$,
\[
L_X(f\circ R_\epsilon)(x)=\nabla f(R_\epsilon x)\cdot R_\epsilon Xx
=(L_Xf)(R_\epsilon x).
\]
By Taylor's formula $h^{-1}(f\circ R_{\epsilon+h}-f\circ R_\epsilon)$
converges uniformly to $(L_Xf)\circ R_\epsilon$ as $h\to0$, with supports
in a fixed cube for $|h|\le1$, so the bound of
Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(i) shows that $F$ is differentiable with
$F'(\epsilon)$ equal to the sum of the lemma at the functions $f_j\circ
R_\epsilon$. If the sum vanishes, $F$ is constant, which is the invariance
for smooth $f_j$, and the bound and the density of $C^\infty_c$ in $C_c$ in
the supremum norm on a cube give it for all $f_j$. Conversely invariance
gives $F'(0)=0$.
\end{proof}

For $X\in\mathfrak{so}(4)$ and $f_j\in C^\infty_c(\R^4)$ put
\[
\Delta^X_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)=\sum_{j=1}^n\omega_k\Bigl(Z_kA_k(L_Xf_j)
\prod_{i\ne j}Z_kA_k(f_i)\Bigr),
\]
the derivative at $\epsilon=0$ of the lattice moment
$\omega_k(\prod_jZ_kA_k(f_j\circ e^{\epsilon X}))$, a finite sum. It
measures the breaking of the rotation by the lattice and by the flow.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:wardform}
Assume the hypotheses of Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:osaxioms}}.

\textup{(i)} $\Delta^X_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)\to0$ for all $n$ and $f_j\in
C^\infty_c(\R^4)$ if and only if every subsequential limit is invariant
under the group $\{e^{\epsilon X}\}$.

\textup{(ii)} Let $X\ne0$ lie in $\mathfrak{so}(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}$, for
instance $X=E_{12}$. Then \textup{(W)} holds if and only if
$\Delta^X_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)\to0$ for all $n$ and $f_j\in C^\infty_c(\R^4)$.

\textup{(iii)} Let $X=E_{01}$. Then $\Delta^X_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)\to0$ for
all $n$ and $f_j\in C^\infty_c(\R^4)$ if and only if every subsequential
limit is invariant under $\mathrm O(4)$. For the family of
Proposition~\textup{\ref{prop:osobstruction}(c)} the identity of
Lemma~\textup{\ref{lem:ward}} with $X=E_{01}$ fails.

\textup{(iv)} If \textup{(T1)--(T3)} hold and $a_k^4W_k\to0$, the
conditions in \textup{(ii)} and \textup{(iii)} need only be imposed for
$n=2$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) Let $S$ be the limit along a subsequence. By
Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(i), applied to the functions $L_Xf_j\in
C^\infty_c$, $\Delta^X_k$ converges along it to the sum of
Lemma~\ref{lem:ward}; if $\Delta^X_k\to0$, the lemma gives the
invariance. If $|\Delta^X_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)|\ge\eta>0$ along a subsequence,
a refinement given by Theorem~\ref{thm:osaxioms}(i) has a limit for which
the sum of Lemma~\ref{lem:ward} is at least $\eta$ in absolute value, and
that limit is not invariant.

(ii) $e^{\epsilon X}$ is the rotation about the line $\ker X$ through an
angle proportional to $\epsilon$, of infinite order for all but countably
many $\epsilon$. If (W) holds, every limit is invariant under $\mathrm
O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$, as stated after (W), and (i) gives $\Delta^X_k\to0$.
Conversely, by (i) each $G_S$ contains a spatial rotation of infinite
order, so $G_S\supseteq\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$ by
Proposition~\ref{prop:rotations}(ii), and (W) follows.

(iii) $e^{\epsilon E_{01}}$ rotates the $(0,1)$ plane, has infinite order
for $\epsilon\notin\pi\mathbb Q$ and moves $e_0$ off $\pm e_0$ for
$\epsilon\notin\pi\Z$; Proposition~\ref{prop:rotations}(iii) and (i) give
the equivalence. The family of Proposition~\ref{prop:osobstruction}(c)
has $G_S=\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$, which does not contain
$e^{\epsilon E_{01}}$ for $\epsilon\notin\pi\Z$, so by
Lemma~\ref{lem:ward} the identity fails for some $f_j$.

(iv) By Theorem~\ref{thm:higher}(v) every limit is Gaussian, so it is
invariant under a rotation if and only if $S_2$ is, and the proofs of (i)
and of Lemma~\ref{lem:ward} apply to $n=2$ alone.
\end{proof}

So the lattice estimate that gives the spatial rotations is
$\Delta^X_k\to0$ for one $X\in\mathfrak{so}(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}$, with no
rate; a bound $|\Delta^X_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)|\le C(f_1,\dots,f_n)a_k^p$ with
$p>0$ would suffice and is not proved. For $X=E_{12}$ the rotation by
$\pi/2$ lies in $W_3$, so $\epsilon\mapsto\omega_k(\prod_jZ_kA_k(f_j\circ
e^{\epsilon X}))$ has period $\pi/2$, and the integral of
$\Delta^X_k(f_1\circ e^{\epsilon X},\dots,f_n\circ e^{\epsilon X})$ over a
period vanishes at every $k$: the lattice symmetry cancels the breaking on
average over the angle, and (ii) asks for the cancellation at each angle
in the limit. For the time--space generators the same identity is the
condition for $\mathrm O(4)$, and part (iii) shows that it fails for the
spatially flowed free field at a positive flow time.

\emph{The four-dimensional flow.} The flow in all four directions
preserves $\mathrm O(4)$, and it loses reflection positivity already for
the free field.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:fourflow}
\textup{(i)} Let $t_f>0$ and $\mu>0$. The centred Gaussian field on $\R^4$
with covariance
\[
\widehat C(p)=e^{-2t_f|p|^2}(|p|^2+\mu^2)^{-1},
\]
the massive free field flowed by the four-dimensional heat flow for the
time $t_f$, is invariant under translations and $\mathrm O(4)$ and is not
reflection positive: there is a real $f\in C^\infty_c(\R^4)$ supported in
$\{t>0\}$ with $\iint f(\theta x)C(x-y)f(y)\,dx\,dy<0$.

\textup{(ii)} Let $S^j$, $j\ge1$, be families as in
Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:osaxioms}(i)--(ii)}, each invariant under
$\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$, bounded by $M_n(Q)\prod_j\|f_j\|_\infty$
uniformly in $j$, and with $S^j_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)\to S^0_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)$
for all $n$ and $f_i\in C_c(\R^4)$. Then $G_{S^0}=\mathrm O(4)$ if and
only if $S^0$ is invariant under the exchange of $e_0$ and $e_1$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) Invariance is clear. Put
\[
H_{\mathbf p}(u)=\frac1{2\pi}\int e^{ip_0u}\widehat C(p_0,\mathbf p)
\,dp_0 ,
\]
an even real function, smooth
in $u$ and continuous in $(\mathbf p,u)$, with $|H_{\mathbf p}(u)|\le
H_{\mathbf p}(0)\le H_0(0)$. For $f=g\otimes h$ with $g$ supported in
$(0,\infty)$ and $g,h$ real, the spatial Fourier transform gives
\[
\begin{split}
\iint f(\theta x)C(x-y)f(y)\,dx\,dy
&=(2\pi)^{-3}\!\int|\hat h(\mathbf p)|^2G_{\mathbf p}\,d\mathbf p,\\
G_{\mathbf p}&=\iint g(s)g(t)H_{\mathbf p}(s+t)\,ds\,dt .
\end{split}
\]
Since $H_0(0)>0$ and $H_0''(0)=-(2\pi)^{-1}\int p_0^2\widehat C(p_0,0)
\,dp_0<0$, $\log H_0$ is strictly concave on an interval $[0,2\delta]$.
For $0<u_1<u_2<\delta$ this gives $H_0(u_1+u_2)^2>H_0(2u_1)H_0(2u_2)$, so
the matrix $(H_0(u_i+u_j))_{i,j\le2}$ has negative determinant and there
is a real $c$ with $\sum_{i,j}c_ic_jH_0(u_i+u_j)<0$. Let $j_\eta\ge0$ be a
mollifier with integral $1$ supported in $[-\eta,\eta]$, $\eta<u_1$, and
$g=c_1j_\eta(\cdot-u_1)+c_2j_\eta(\cdot-u_2)$; for small $\eta$,
$G_0<0$ by continuity of $H_0$. Fix such $g$; then $\mathbf p\mapsto
G_{\mathbf p}$ is continuous and bounded by $\|g\|_1^2H_0(0)$. For a real
nonzero $h_1\in C^\infty_c(\R^3)$ and $h_L(\mathbf x)=L^{-3/2}h_1(\mathbf
x/L)$, the right side for $h=h_L$ is $(2\pi)^{-3}\int|\hat h_1(\mathbf
q)|^2G_{\mathbf q/L}\,d\mathbf q$, which tends to $\|h_1\|_2^2G_0<0$ as
$L\to\infty$ by dominated convergence.

(ii) The bound makes each condition $S^0_n(f_1\circ R^{-1},\dots)=
S^0_n(f_1,\dots)$ a limit of the same conditions for $S^j$, so
$G_{S^0}\supseteq\mathrm O(3)\times\mathrm O(1)$, which is infinite. The
exchange $g$ has $ge_0=e_1$, and Proposition~\ref{prop:rotations}(iii)
gives the claim; the converse is clear.
\end{proof}

At $t_f=0$ the function $H_{\mathbf p}(u)=e^{-\omega|u|}/(2\omega)$ is
log-linear on $(0,\infty)$, and the field is reflection positive. The
proof of (i) shows that reflection positivity needs the matrices
$(H_{\mathbf p}(u_i+u_j))$ with $u_i>0$ to be positive semidefinite, which
makes $H_{\mathbf p}$ log-convex on $(0,\infty)$; a smooth even function
with $H''(0)<0$ is not log-convex near $0$. So at a positive
flow time the spatial flow keeps reflection positivity and loses
$\mathrm O(4)$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:osobstruction}(c)), and the
four-dimensional flow keeps $\mathrm O(4)$ and loses reflection
positivity. By part (ii), at vanishing flow time, for limits $S^0$ of
families that satisfy (W) at positive flow times with uniform bounds,
$\mathrm O(4)$ reduces to one discrete symmetry, the exchange of time with
a spatial axis, which the unflowed lattice theory has; the interchange of
the limits $k\to\infty$ and $t_f\to0$ that would carry it to $S^0$, and the
renormalisation of the fields as $t_f\to0$, are not treated here.

\emph{One limit under a renormalisation condition.} Proposition
\ref{prop:uniquelimit}(iii) uses the convergence of one ratio. Choosing the
couplings so that it holds is a renormalisation condition:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(RC)},leftmargin=*]
\item There are a dyadic $\sigma_0>0$ and $\rho_*\in(0,1)$ with
$\rho_k(0,\sigma_0)\to\rho_*$.
\end{enumerate}
That a tuning with (RC) exists is not proved. Since (RC) contains
$(\mathrm N_0)$, it is not weaker than the open input of
Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit}.

Under (M), let $\mathfrak X$ be the set of families $S=(S_n)$ of
multilinear forms on $C_c(\R^4)$ with $|S_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)|\le M_n(Q)
\prod_j\|f_j\|_\infty$ for $f_j$ supported in a cube $Q$. For $L\in\mathbb
N$ let $\mathcal F_L$ be a countable $\mathbb Q(i)$-linear set of
functions supported in $[-L,L]^4$, dense there in the supremum norm, let
$P_1,P_2,\dots$ enumerate the tuples of elements of
$\bigcup_L\mathcal F_L$, and put $d(S,S')=\sum_i2^{-i}\min(1,|S(P_i)-
S'(P_i)|)$. By the bounds and density, $d(S^m,S)\to0$ if and only if
$S^m_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)\to S_n(f_1,\dots,f_n)$ for all $n$ and $f_j\in
C_c(\R^4)$, and by the diagonal argument of Theorem~\ref{thm:higher}(ii)
$(\mathfrak X,d)$ is a compact metric space. For $k\ge k_0$ the lattice
moments $x_k=(\omega_k(\prod_jZ_kA_k(f_j)))_n$ lie in $\mathfrak X$; let
$\mathfrak L$ be their set of limit points, the set of subsequential
limits, and put
\[
\begin{split}
\delta_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)&=\omega_{k+1}\Bigl(\prod_jZ_{k+1}A_{k+1}(f_j)
\Bigr)\\
&\quad-\omega_k\Bigl(\prod_jZ_kA_k(f_j)\Bigr).
\end{split}
\]

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:limitset}
If $(x_k)$ is a sequence in a compact metric space with
$d(x_k,x_{k+1})\to0$, its set of limit points is nonempty and connected.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The set $L$ of limit points is nonempty and closed. If $L=A\cup B$ with
$A,B$ disjoint, closed and nonempty, let $3\eta$ be their distance and
$A_\eta,B_\eta$ their open $\eta$-neighbourhoods, at distance at least
$\eta$. The sequence enters both infinitely often, and once
$d(x_k,x_{k+1})<\eta$ it cannot pass from one to the other in one step, so
infinitely many $x_k$ lie in the compact complement of $A_\eta\cup
B_\eta$, which then contains a limit point. That contradicts $L\subseteq
A\cup B$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:tuned}
\textup{(i)} \textup{(RC)} implies $(\mathrm N_u)$ for every dyadic
$u\ge0$, and every limit of Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:flowlimit}} along a
subsequence is nontrivial and has $\rho(\sigma_0)=\rho_*$. Under the
hypothesis of Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:transfer}}, $m_*\le\sigma_0^{-1}
\log(1/\rho_*)$. For $\SU(2)$, \textup{(RC)} implies $\beta_{W,k}>0.348$
for all large $k$. Under \textup{(M)}, every $S\in\mathfrak L$ has
$S_2(\bar f_0\circ\theta,f_0(\cdot-(\sigma_0,0)))=\rho_*$.

\textup{(ii)} Assume the hypotheses of
Theorem~\textup{\ref{thm:osaxioms}}. Then \textup{(U)} holds if and only if
the following hold:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(U\arabic*)},leftmargin=*]
\item $\delta_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)\to0$ for all $n$ and $f_j\in C_c(\R^4)$;
\item $\mathfrak L$ is totally disconnected.
\end{enumerate}

\textup{(iii)} Under the same hypotheses, if
$\sum_k|\delta_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)|<\infty$ for all $n$ and all $f_j$ in a
set whose linear span is dense in the continuous functions supported in
each cube, then \textup{(U)} holds.

\textup{(iv)} Let $m>0$, $q_k=e^{-ma_k}$, $\varepsilon_k=2+\sin(\log k)$,
$K_\varepsilon(\mathbf z)=e^{-|\mathbf z|^2/\varepsilon}$ on $\R^3$, let
$\phi_k$ be the centred Gaussian field on $\Z^4$ with covariance
\[
q_k^{|x_0-y_0|}K_{\varepsilon_k}\bigl(a_k(\mathbf x-\mathbf y)\bigr),
\]
$A_k(f)=\sum_xa_k^4f(a_kx)\phi_k(x)$, and $f_0\ge0$ nonzero. The law of
$\phi_k$ is invariant under translations and $\Gamma$ and reflection
positive for the time reflections through sites and bisecting links, and
its transfer matrix has gap $ma_k$. Moreover $\rho_k(u,\sigma)=
e^{-m\sigma}$ for all $u,\sigma$, so \textup{(RC)} holds for every
$\sigma_0$; \textup{(T1)--(T3)} hold with $r_k=1$ for large $k$, and
\textup{(M)}, \textup{(W)} and \textup{(U1)} hold, with
$|\delta_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)|\le C/k$ for $f_j\in C^1_c(\R^4)$. But
$\mathfrak L$ is homeomorphic to $[1,3]$, and \textup{(U)} fails.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) The first claims are Proposition~\ref{prop:ratioexact}(iii) and
Theorem~\ref{thm:flowlimit}(ii). Under the hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer} the gap is at least $m_*a_k$ on
$\Omega_k^\perp$, and as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:strongratio}
$\rho_k(0,\sigma_0)\le e^{-m_*\sigma_0}$, so $\rho_*\le e^{-m_*\sigma_0}$.
The statement for $\SU(2)$ is Theorem~\ref{thm:strongratio}. The last
claim is the computation in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:uniquelimit}(iii).

(ii) If (U) holds, $x_k$ converges in $\mathfrak X$ by
Proposition~\ref{prop:uniquelimit}(i), so $d(x_k,x_{k+1})\to0$, which is
(U1), and $\mathfrak L$ is a point. Conversely (U1) is $d(x_k,x_{k+1})\to
0$, so $\mathfrak L$ is connected by Lemma~\ref{lem:limitset}; a
nonempty, connected and totally disconnected set is a point, and (U)
follows.

(iii) The moments of tuples from the set converge, being Cauchy; by
multilinearity so do those of tuples from its span, and by (M) and density
all moments converge. Proposition~\ref{prop:uniquelimit}(i) gives (U).

(iv) $K_\varepsilon$ is positive definite on $\R^3$, its Fourier
transform being positive, hence on $a_k\Z^3$, and the field is the
stationary Markov chain of the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:osobstruction}(a) with $\gamma$ the Gaussian law of
covariance $K_{\varepsilon_k}(a_k(\mathbf x-\mathbf y))$; this gives
reflection positivity, the gap and, since $K_\varepsilon$ is radial, the
invariance. As in Proposition~\ref{prop:msplit}(vi), the time factor of
the covariance gives $C_k(2u+\sigma)=e^{-m\sigma}C_k(2u)$. The kernel
$k_\varepsilon(z)=e^{-m|z_0|}K_\varepsilon(\mathbf z)$ is bounded by $1$
and Lipschitz, uniformly in $\varepsilon\in[1,3]$, and the covariance of
$A_k(f)$ and $A_k(f')$ is a Riemann sum of mesh $a_k$ for $\iint
f(x)f'(y)k_{\varepsilon_k}(x-y)\,dx\,dy$, with error at most $C(f,f')a_k$
for $f,f'\in C^1_c$ and tending to $0$ uniformly in $\varepsilon$ for
$f,f'\in C_c$. In particular $C_k(0)$ differs by $o(1)$ from
$c(\varepsilon_k)$, where $c(\varepsilon)=\iint f_0(\theta x)f_0(y)
k_\varepsilon(x-y)\,dx\,dy$ is positive and continuous on $[1,3]$. With
$w_k(z)=q_k^{|z_0|}e^{-a_k^2|\mathbf z|^2/3}$, which bounds the covariance
since $\varepsilon_k\le3$, $a_k^4W_k\to(2/m)(3\pi)^{3/2}$, and (T1) holds
with $K_2=1$, $K_n=0$ for $n\ge3$, (T2) for large $k$, and (T3);
Theorem~\ref{thm:ostree}(i) gives (M). Let $S^\varepsilon$ be the
Gaussian family with
\[
S^\varepsilon_2(f,f')=c(\varepsilon)^{-1}\iint f(x)f'(y)
k_\varepsilon(x-y)\,dx\,dy .
\]
By Wick's rule, $|x_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)-S^{\varepsilon_k}(f_1,\dots,f_n)|$
tends to $0$, and is at most $Ca_k$ for $f_j\in C^1_c$. The derivative of
$k_\varepsilon$ in $\varepsilon$ is bounded on compact sets uniformly in
$\varepsilon\in[1,3]$ and $c>0$ there, so $|S^\varepsilon(f_1,\dots,f_n)-
S^{\varepsilon'}(f_1,\dots,f_n)|\le C|\varepsilon-\varepsilon'|$, and
$|\varepsilon_{k+1}-\varepsilon_k|\le\log(1+1/k)\le1/k$; this gives the
bound on $\delta_k$, and (U1) follows by approximation and (M). Since $\log
k\to\infty$ with increments tending to $0$, the limit points of
$(\varepsilon_k)$ form $[1,3]$, so $\mathfrak L=\{S^\varepsilon:1\le
\varepsilon\le3\}$. Each $S^\varepsilon$ is invariant under $\mathrm O(3)
\times\mathrm O(1)$, since $k_\varepsilon$ is, which gives (W). If
$S^\varepsilon_2=S^{\varepsilon'}_2$, the continuous kernels
$k_\varepsilon/c(\varepsilon)$ and $k_{\varepsilon'}/c(\varepsilon')$
coincide; at $z=0$ this gives $c(\varepsilon)=c(\varepsilon')$, and then
$K_\varepsilon=K_{\varepsilon'}$, so $\varepsilon=\varepsilon'$. So
$\varepsilon\mapsto S^\varepsilon$ is a continuous injection of $[1,3]$
onto $\mathfrak L$, a homeomorphism.
\end{proof}

So (RC) fixes the value of one functional on every limit and leaves (U1)
and (U2) open: example (iv) satisfies (RC), (M), (W), the tree bounds and
(U1), and its limits form an interval; the same field with
$\varepsilon_k$ alternating between $1$ and $3$ satisfies (RC), (M),
(W), the tree bounds and (U2), and has two limits. Part (iii) is the form
that a scale-by-scale analysis would supply. Along the one-loop tuning of
Proposition~\ref{prop:scalelosses}, $\beta_k=hk+O(1)$, so a bound
$|\delta_k(f_1,\dots,f_n)|\le C/\beta_k$, of first order in the running
coupling, is of the size $C/k$ of example (iv) and does not imply (U),
while a bound $C\beta_k^{-p}$ with $p>1$ is summable and gives (U) by
(iii). As for the ratio in Proposition~\ref{prop:scalelosses}, a bound on
the change of the moments from one scale to the next gives (U) only if it
is better than first order in the running coupling, or if the changes
cancel between scales.

\begin{remark}\label{rem:rotatedlattice}
Let $f_0$ be invariant under the spatial rotations and
$R\in\SO(3)_{\mathrm{sp}}$. The moments $x^R_k=(\omega_k(\prod_jZ_kA_k(f_j
\circ R^{-1})))_n$ are those of the theory on the rotated lattice
$R(a_k\Z^4)$, whose packet of $f_0$ is $A_k$; it has the same $C_k$, $Z_k$
and $\rho_k$, and satisfies (RC) when the original theory does. The
sequence $x_1,x^R_1,x_2,x^R_2,\dots$ converges if and only if (U) holds
and the difference in (W) for $R$ tends to $0$: if it converges, both
subsequences have the same limit, and conversely. For $R$ of infinite order
this is (U) and (W) together, the statement that the limit does not depend
on which of the lattices $R(a_k\Z^4)$ is used. Example (a) of
Proposition~\ref{prop:osobstruction} satisfies (RC), since its time
dependence factorises as in Proposition~\ref{prop:msplit}(vi), as well as
(M), (U) and the tree bounds, and not (W); example (iv) of
Proposition~\ref{prop:tuned} satisfies (RC), (M), (W) and the tree
bounds, and not (U).
\end{remark}

\emph{The time direction and the slice field.} The two parts of (M) can be
compared with correlations of slice fields, as the ratio was in
Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}. In the time direction the comparison is
exact, because the transfer matrix is positive. In the spatial directions no
such comparison is available: the spatial flow acts within each time slice
and mixes the two sides of every spatial reflection plane. For
$h'\in C_c(\R^3)$ put
\[
U^{h'}_k=\sum_{x\in\Z^3}a_k^3h'(a_kx)\bigl(\varphi_k(0,x)-\omega_k(
\varphi_k(0,x))\bigr),
\]
and, for $r\in a_k\Z_{\ge0}$ and $\delta>0$,
\[
\begin{split}
D^{h'}_k(r)&=\omega_k\bigl(\bar U^{h'}_k\,\tau_{r/a_k}U^{h'}_k\bigr),\\
I^{h'}_k(\delta)&=a_k\!\!\sum_{r\in a_k\Z,\,0\le r<\delta}\!\!D^{h'}_k(r).
\end{split}
\]
For the spatial factor $h$ of a product packet, $U^h_k=U_k$ and
$D^h_k=D_k$ are those of Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}; write
$I_k=I^h_k$. As in the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:msplit}(iv),
$D^{h'}_k(r)=\ip u{\mathbb T_k^{r/a_k}u}$ with $u=\Psi_{U^{h'}_k}$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:timekernel}
Let $\mathbb T$ be self-adjoint with $0\le\mathbb T\le1$ on a Hilbert space,
let $v_n$, $n\in\Z$, be vectors, nonzero only for $n$ in a set of $N$
consecutive integers, put $D_n(j)=\ip{v_n}{\mathbb T^jv_n}$ and
\[
B=\sum_{n,n'\in\Z}\ip{v_n}{\mathbb T^{|n-n'|}v_{n'}} .
\]

\textup{(i)} $|B|\le2N\max_n\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}D_n(j)$.

\textup{(ii)} If $v_n=w_nv$ with $w_n\ge0$, and $w_n\ge\eta$ on a set of
$M\ge1$ consecutive integers, then, with $D(j)=\ip v{\mathbb T^jv}$,
\[
B\ge\eta^2M\sum_{j=0}^{\lceil M/2\rceil-1}D(j) .
\]

\textup{(iii)} $D(j)$ is nonnegative and nonincreasing in $j$, and
$\sum_{j<pq}D(j)\le q\sum_{j<p}D(j)$ for integers $p,q\ge1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) Since $\mathbb T^j\ge0$, the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality for the form
$\ip\cdot{\mathbb T^j\cdot}$ gives $|\ip{v_n}{\mathbb T^jv_{n'}}|\le
\frac12(D_n(j)+D_{n'}(j))$. Summing over $n,n'$ and using the symmetry,
$|B|\le\sum_n\sum_{n'}D_n(|n-n'|)$, and for fixed $n$ the integers $n'$
with $v_{n'}\ne0$ give each value of $|n-n'|$ at most twice, all values
below $N$.

(ii) All terms of $B$ are nonnegative, since $D\ge0$. Keep the pairs in the
set $J$ of $M$ consecutive integers. An element of $J$ has at least
$\lceil M/2\rceil-1$ further elements of $J$ on one of its sides, so its row
contributes at least $\eta^2\sum_{j<\lceil M/2\rceil}D(j)$.

(iii) $D(j)=\int\lambda^jd\nu$ for the spectral measure $\nu$ of
$\mathbb T$ at $v$, carried by $[0,1]$. Each of the $q$ blocks of $p$
consecutive terms is at most the first block.
\end{proof}

By (iii), $D^{h'}_k$ is nonincreasing and $I^{h'}_k(p\delta)\le
pI^{h'}_k(\delta)$ for integers $p\ge1$ and $\delta\in a_k\Z$. By (i) and
(ii), with $v_n=\Psi_{U^{h'}_k}$ for $0\le a_kn<\delta$, the number
$\delta^{-1}I^{h'}_k(\delta)$ lies between one half and twice the variance
of the average of $\tau_nU^{h'}_k$ over these $n$: $I^{h'}_k(\delta)$ is the
variance of the slice field averaged over a physical time $\delta$, times
$\delta$. In the rest of this paragraph the states are invariant under
translations and $\vartheta$ and reflection positive, with the transfer
matrices of Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}, $\Phi$ is real, and $f_0=g\otimes h$
is a product packet as in Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}, with an interval
$I$ of length $\ell_I>0$ and $c_g>0$ such that $g\ge c_g$ on $I$, as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:msplit}(iv).

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:mzslice}
Let $\delta>0$ be dyadic and let $k$ be so large that $\delta/a_k\in\Z$ and
$a_k\le\min(\ell_I/4,\delta)$.

\textup{(i)} Put $T_g=t_1-t_0+\delta$ and
\[
\begin{split}
c_-(\delta)&=\frac{3c_g^2\ell_I}{4\lceil8\delta/(3\ell_I)\rceil},\qquad
c_+(\delta)=2\|g\|_\infty^2T_g\Bigl\lceil\frac{T_g}\delta\Bigr\rceil.
\end{split}
\]
Then $c_-(\delta)I_k(\delta)\le V_k\le c_+(\delta)I_k(\delta)$ and
\[
\bigl(\tfrac34c_g\ell_I\bigr)^2D_k(2a_kn_1)\le C_k(0)\le
\|g\|_\infty^2T_g^2\,D_k(2a_kn_0) .
\]

\textup{(ii)} Consider
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(Z$_{\mathrm{sl}}$)},leftmargin=*]
\item There are a dyadic $\delta>0$ and $C<\infty$ with
$I_k(\delta)\le C\,D_k(\delta)$ for all large $k$.
\end{enumerate}
If \textup{(Z$_{\mathrm{sl}}$)} holds for one dyadic $\delta$, it holds for
every dyadic $\delta>0$, and then $D_k(\delta)\ge(\delta/(2C))D_k(\delta/2)$
and $a_kD_k(0)\le C\,D_k(\delta)$ for all large $k$.

\textup{(iii)} \textup{(M$_Z$)} holds if and only if
\textup{(Z$_{\mathrm{sl}}$)} holds. In particular \textup{(M$_Z$)} holds for
one product packet with spatial factor $h$ if and only if it holds for every
product packet $g'\otimes h$ with $g'$ as above, and it implies
$(\mathrm N_u)$ for every dyadic $u\ge0$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(i) By time translation invariance, $A_k=\sum_nw_n\tau_nU_k$ with
$w_n=a_kg(a_kn)$, and $V_k=\sum_{n,n'}w_nw_{n'}D_k(a_k|n-n'|)$ by the
identity of the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:msplit}(iv). The $n$ with
$w_n\ne0$ lie in $[n_0,n_1]$, a set of $N\le(t_1-t_0)/a_k+1$ integers, so
$a_kN\le T_g$. Lemma~\ref{lem:timekernel}(i) with $v_n=w_nu_k$ and
$|w_n|\le a_k\|g\|_\infty$ gives
\[
V_k\le2\|g\|_\infty^2(a_kN)\,I_k(a_kN)\le2\|g\|_\infty^2T_g\Bigl\lceil
\frac{T_g}\delta\Bigr\rceil I_k(\delta) ,
\]
by (iii) of the lemma. The set $J$ of $n$ with $a_kn\in I$ consists of
$M\ge\ell_I/a_k-1\ge\frac34\ell_I/a_k$ consecutive integers, and $w_n\ge
a_kc_g$ there. Lemma~\ref{lem:timekernel}(ii) gives, with
$b=a_k\lceil M/2\rceil\ge\frac38\ell_I$,
\[
V_k\ge a_k^2c_g^2M\!\!\sum_{j<\lceil M/2\rceil}\!\!D_k(a_kj)
=c_g^2(a_kM)\,I_k(b)\ge\tfrac34c_g^2\ell_I\,I_k(b),
\]
and $I_k(\delta)\le\lceil\delta/b\rceil I_k(b)\le\lceil8\delta/(3\ell_I)
\rceil I_k(b)$. The bounds on $C_k(0)$ are those of the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}(i), $C_k(0)=\int W^2d\nu$ with
$\|w\|_1\lambda^{n_1}\le W(\lambda)\le\|w\|_1\lambda^{n_0}$, together with
$\|w\|_1\ge a_kc_gM\ge\frac34c_g\ell_I$ and $\|w\|_1\le a_kN\|g\|_\infty
\le T_g\|g\|_\infty$.

(ii) Since $D_k$ is nonincreasing, $I_k(\delta)\ge\frac\delta2D_k(\delta/2)$
and $I_k(\delta)\ge a_kD_k(0)$, which gives the two bounds; in particular
$c=\delta/(2C)\le1$, because $D_k(\delta)\le D_k(\delta/2)$ and
$D_k(\delta/2)>0$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}(i). Let
$\delta'$ be dyadic. If $\delta'<\delta$, then $I_k(\delta')\le
I_k(\delta)\le CD_k(\delta)\le CD_k(\delta')$. If $\delta'>\delta$,
Proposition~\ref{prop:sliceratio}(ii) with $r=\delta/2$, $r'=\delta$ gives
$D_k(\delta')\ge c^{e}D_k(\delta)$ with $e=\max(1,2(\delta'-\delta)/\delta)$,
and
\[
I_k(\delta')\le I_k(\delta)+(\delta'-\delta)D_k(\delta)\le
(C+\delta')\,c^{-e}D_k(\delta') .
\]

(iii) Suppose $\Upsilon_k\le\Upsilon$ for all $k$, and take a dyadic
$\delta\le2t_0$. Then $2a_kn_0\ge2t_0\ge\delta$, and by (i)
\[
I_k(\delta)\le\frac{V_k}{c_-(\delta)}\le\frac{\Upsilon C_k(0)}{c_-(\delta)}
\le\frac{\Upsilon\|g\|_\infty^2T_g^2}{c_-(\delta)}D_k(\delta) ,
\]
which is (Z$_{\mathrm{sl}}$). Conversely, assume (Z$_{\mathrm{sl}}$) at
$\delta$ with the constant $C$, and take a dyadic $R\ge\max(2t_1,\delta)$.
Then $D_k(2a_kn_1)\ge D_k(R)\ge c^eD_k(\delta)$ with $c=\delta/(2C)$ and
$e=\max(1,2(R-\delta)/\delta)$, as in (ii), and by (i)
\[
\Upsilon_k=\frac{V_k}{C_k(0)}\le\frac{c_+(\delta)\,C\,c^{-e}}
{(\frac34c_g\ell_I)^2}
\]
for all large $k$; the finitely many other $\Upsilon_k$ are finite, since
$C_k(0)>0$. Condition (Z$_{\mathrm{sl}}$) involves $h$ and not $g$, and
(M$_Z$) implies $(\mathrm N_u)$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:msplit}(iv).
\end{proof}

So for product packets (M$_Z$) is a statement about the slice field alone:
the variance of $U_k$ averaged over a physical time $\delta$ is at most a
constant times $D_k(\delta)/\delta$. Since $I_k(\delta)\ge a_kD_k(0)$, it
contains the bound $a_k\Var U_k\le C\,D_k(\delta)$: the equal-time variance
of the slice field may exceed its correlation at a physical separation by
at most a factor of order $1/a_k$. In terms of the spectral measure
$\mu_k$ of $\mathbb T_k$ at $u_k$, in energies $E=-a_k^{-1}\log\lambda$,
$I_k(\delta)$ is the integral of $a_k(1-e^{-\delta E})/(1-e^{-a_kE})$, a
weight between $(1-e^{-\delta E})/E$ and that plus $a_k$, and
(Z$_{\mathrm{sl}}$) bounds it by $\int e^{-\delta E}d\mu_k$; it bounds the
spectral weight at high energy, counted with the factor $1/E$, by the
weight at bounded physical energy. By (ii) it contains a ratio bound for
$D_k$, hence $(\mathrm N_0)$, and it is strictly stronger than
$(\mathrm N_u)$: for the field of Proposition~\ref{prop:msplit}(vi),
$\rho_k(u,\sigma)=e^{-m\sigma}$ while $a_kD_k(0)$, which contains
$a_k\cdot a_k^{-5}a_k^6\sum_xh(a_kx)^2$, grows like $a_k^{-1}$, and
$D_k(\delta)$ converges. Along the constant tunings of
Proposition~\ref{prop:cubecompatible}(ii) the hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer} holds and (Z$_{\mathrm{sl}}$) fails, since
$(\mathrm N_u)$ fails there. So (M$_Z$) reduces to $(\mathrm N_u)$ in one
direction only, and no argument that uses (D) only through the hypothesis of
Theorem~\ref{thm:transfer} gives it.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:mlocslice}
Let $\delta$ and $k$ be as in Proposition~\textup{\ref{prop:mzslice}}.

\textup{(i)} Let $g_1,g_2\in C_c(\R)$ be supported in an interval of length
$L$, $y_1,y_2\in a_k\Z^3$ and $f_i=g_i\otimes h(\cdot-y_i)$. Then
\[
\bigl|\omega_k\bigl(A_k(f_1)A_k(f_2)\bigr)\bigr|\le K_L\,V_k\,
\|g_1\|_\infty\|g_2\|_\infty ,
\]
with $K_L=2(L+\delta)\lceil(L+\delta)/\delta\rceil/c_-(\delta)$. So
\textup{(M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$)} holds for $n=2$ on these products, along every
sequence of couplings.

\textup{(ii)} \textup{(M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$)} holds for $n=2$ if and only if
the following holds for one dyadic $\delta>0$, and then it holds for every
dyadic $\delta>0$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(M$_{\mathrm{sp}}$)},leftmargin=*]
\item For every cube $Q_3\subset\R^3$ there is $C<\infty$ with
$I^{h'}_k(\delta)\le C\,I_k(\delta)$ for all large $k$ and all
$h'\in C_c(\R^3)$ supported in $Q_3$ with $\|h'\|_\infty\le1$.
\end{enumerate}

\textup{(iii)} \textup{(M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$)} holds if and only if for every
even $n\ge2$ and every cube $Q$ there are $C_n(Q)$ and $k_0$ with
$\omega_k(|A_k(f)|^n)\le C_n(Q)V_k^{n/2}\|f\|_\infty^n$ for $k\ge k_0$ and
$f\in C_c(\R^4)$ supported in $Q$. The case $n=2$ is equivalent to
\textup{(M$_{\mathrm{sp}}$)}, and the case $n$ implies the cases below it.

\textup{(iv)} Let $m>0$, $q_k=e^{-ma_k}$, $\kappa(\mathbf z)=e^{-|\mathbf
z|^2}$ on $\R^3$, and let $\psi_k$ be the centred Gaussian field on $\Z^4$
with covariance
\[
q_k^{|x_0-y_0|}\Bigl(\kappa\bigl(a_k(\mathbf x-\mathbf y)\bigr)+a_k^{-1}
(-1)^{\sum_{i=1}^3(x_i-y_i)}\Bigr),
\]
$A_k(f)=\sum_xa_k^4f(a_kx)\psi_k(x)$, and $f_0=g\otimes h$ a product packet as in
Proposition~\textup{\ref{prop:sliceratio}} with $g,h\ge0$ nonzero and
continuously differentiable. The law of $\psi_k$ is invariant
under translations and $\Gamma$ and reflection positive for the time
reflections through sites and bisecting links, its transfer matrix has gap
$ma_k$, $\rho_k(u,\sigma)=e^{-m\sigma}$ for all $u,\sigma$, so \textup{(RC)}
holds for every $\sigma_0$, and \textup{(M$_Z$)} holds. But
\textup{(M$_{\mathrm{sp}}$)} fails, and \textup{(M$_{\mathrm{loc}}$)} and
\textup{(M)} fail for $n=2$.

\textup{(v)} Let $m>0$, $q_k=e^{-ma_k}$, $\epsilon_k=a_k$, let
$(\xi_n)_{n\in\Z}$ be the stationary Markov chain on $\{-1,0,1\}$ with the
law $\pi$ of Remark~\textup{\ref{rem:markov}} at $\epsilon=\epsilon_k$ and
transition operator $P=q_kI+(1-q_k)\Pi$, and $X_n=\xi_n/\sqrt{\epsilon_k}$.
